Incremental Computation for Efficient Programmable Inference in Probabilistic Programs DOI: 10.1145/3808316 Journal: PACMPL Volume: 10 PLDI 238 6 pldi26main-p491-p CCS: Mathematics of computing Probabilistic reasoning algorithms CCS: Theory of computation Denotational semantics CCS: Mathematics of computing Bayesian computation
Fabian Zaiser, Jack Czenszak, Martin C. Rinard, Vikash K. Mansinghka, Alexander K. Lew

Abstract
. Inference in probabilistic programs generally requires evaluating many possible program executions to find those of high posterior density. To scale inference to large datasets, it is crucial that expensive intermediate results are shared across these many evaluations, rather than recomputed from scratch. This paper presents a new approach to realizing this sharing, based on incremental computation , a technique for efficiently recomputing (deterministic) program outputs when program inputs change. First, we show how expressive probabilistic programs can be compiled to deterministic ones that compute their density functions. Then, building on the incremental λ \lambda -calculus, we develop a general technique for compositionally incrementalizing expressive functional programs, and apply it to these densities. The resulting incremental densities can be used to accelerate a broad range of Monte Carlo inference algorithms, including for nonparametric models not well supported by existing systems. Furthermore, our decomposition of incremental density computation into separate density and incrementalization steps allows for modular reasoning about correctness—a key pain point in existing systems, where ad-hoc incrementalization features are a known source of soundness bugs. We develop denotational logical relations arguments for the correctness of each step independently, and implement the approach in a Julia prototype, finding that it leads to asymptotic runtime improvements in the size of the dataset on a range of models and inference algorithms.

1.
Introduction

Probabilistic programming systems promise to accelerate the practice of probabilistic modeling and inference, by providing languages for expressing rich probabilistic models, and automation for aspects of inference.
Unfortunately, for many models, probabilistic programming systems produce slow inference code—in some cases, asymptotically slower than expert implementations of the same algorithms.
One culprit behind this efficiency gap is insufficient
incrementalization
: inference can require thousands or millions of queries to the probability density function of a model, and it is crucial for scalability that expensive intermediate results are shared across these queries.

In this paper, we propose a new approach to closing this efficiency gap for a broad class of models and Monte Carlo inference algorithms, based on
incremental computation
—a technique for efficiently recomputing (deterministic) program outputs when program inputs change. As illustrated in
Fig.

1
, we first show how expressive probabilistic programs can be compiled into deterministic programs that compute their density functions. Then, building on the incremental
\lambda
-calculus
(
18
;
9
)
, we develop a general technique for compositionally incrementalizing expressive higher-order functional programs, and apply it to these density functions.

Our approach addresses two key weaknesses of existing incremental features in probabilistic programming systems

(
13
;
29
;
45
;
31
;
39
;
10
)
. First, existing incrementalization is often limited to particular inference algorithms (e.g., single-site Metropolis-Hastings) or model classes (e.g., parametric models with a fixed number of random variables). By contrast, our approach treats densities as ordinary deterministic programs, and incrementalizes them with respect to arbitrary input changes. As a result, we support the incrementalization needs of a broad range of inference algorithms (
Fig.

2(e)
), including for nonparametric models such as Dirichlet process mixtures. Second, incrementalization in the presence of probability is notoriously difficult to get right, and existing implementations are brittle and prone to soundness bugs (see, e.g.,
Fig.

2(g)
). Our staged approach isolates all probabilistic reasoning in the density compilation step, so that incrementalization need only be proven correct for a deterministic language. We develop new techniques for carrying out this proof: a new approach to typed incrementalization of closures, and a coinductive
updater
abstraction that encapsulates cached intermediate values and supports iterated sequences of updates, enabling a novel denotational correctness argument for caching-based higher-order incremental computation. Our approach is implemented in a Julia prototype that achieves asymptotic speedups over Gen, a state-of-the-art incrementalizing PPL, across a range of models and inference algorithms.

Figure 2
.
Example: modeling and inference for a 2D Gaussian mixture model with incremental densities.

To illustrate our approach, we consider the problem of inferring a
clustering
for a dataset of points
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
in the plane (
Fig.

2(a)
). Our first step is to write a probabilistic program,
\mathsf{gmm}
(
Fig.

2(b)
), encoding a
generative model
: a hypothesized data-generating process by which we imagine our dataset might have been constructed. The program
\mathsf{gmm}
(for “Gaussian mixture model”) accepts as input a number
n
of datapoints to generate, and outputs a synthetic dataset: a list of
n
pairs. As the simulated dataset in
Fig.

2(c)
illustrates, the key feature of this program is that it generates datasets in which points tend to cluster into a handful of ellipse-shaped components. Indeed, in each simulation, the program explicitly chooses a random number of components to generate, samples random parameters for each component, and randomly chooses a component from which to generate each datapoint. Thus, we can reframe the problem of
inferring a clustering
for our dataset as the problem of finding
executions
of the
\mathsf{gmm}
program that could plausibly have generated it.

Probabilistic Programs and Traces.

The
\mathsf{gmm}
program consists of a sequence of
random assignment
statements
\mathsf{x}\sim\mathsf{t}
, with an identifier
\mathsf{x}
on the left and a probabilistic expression
\mathsf{t}
on the right (i.e., an expression whose meaning is a probability distribution that the system can sample). A
trace
of a probabilistic program is a record of a particular execution: for each random assignment
\mathsf{x}\sim\mathsf{t}
, it stores an entry
\textnormal{x:}\,v
, recording the choice(s)
v
made by
\mathsf{t}
during that execution. For example, consider the trace in
Fig.

2(c)
. The first line of
\mathsf{gmm}
draws a random number from a geometric distribution and generates a set of that many fresh
names
; the trace records that this set,
clusterNames
, was
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4453,0.6992}\mathsf{id}_{73}},{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.625,0}\mathsf{id}_{64}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6211,0.4492}\mathsf{id}_{12}},{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.4727,0.6563}\mathsf{id}_{30}}\}
, of size
4
. Next,
\mathsf{gmm}
loops over this set and for each name inside, generates a
covariance
and
mean
from particular
prior distributions
. Because this loop is over an unordered
set
of names, its trace (stored under the label “
params
”) is an unordered
map
from names to the values sampled during the corresponding iteration of the loop. Next, the
\mathsf{gmm}
program uses the
\mathsf{dirichlet}
primitive to sample random
mixing weights
, resulting in a map from cluster names to real-valued weights, summing to 1. The program then uses the
\mathsf{multinomial}
primitive to draw a list of
n

cluster assignments
according to the mixing weights. Finally, the program loops through this list of assignments to generate the
n
datapoints. Each datapoint is generated using the
\mathsf{normal}
primitive, with parameters determined by the component to which we have assigned the point. Because this loop is over an (ordered) list of
n
elements, its trace is also a list of
n
elements, recording the values sampled in each iteration of the loop.

Density Functions.

The
density function
of a probabilistic program maps an execution trace to a non-negative score, which intuitively captures how likely the trace is. In
Section

3
, we present a program transformation that automatically compiles probabilistic programs to deterministic ones that compute their density functions.
Roughly speaking, the density of a trace can be computed by running the program as usual, except instead of sampling from a primitive distribution, one looks up the recorded value in the trace and multiplies a running accumulator (the
weight
) by the (known) probability mass or density of the recorded value under the primitive distribution.
1
1

1

This is a standard technique. However, since our language supports fresh name generation and unordered collections, there are several subtleties that complicate the derivation of density functions. We address these challenges in
Section

3
.

For the
\mathsf{gmm}
model, the value of the density function is that it helps distinguish good clusterings from bad ones: if we fix the
data
entry to match our observed dataset, and vary the number of clusters, their parameters, and their assignments, the density will be low when the trace is incoherent, and high when it provides a plausible explanation of the data.

Incremental Density Computation.

Inferring a plausible trace consistent with observed data typically requires the density function to be evaluated at many related traces. The goal of this paper is to accelerate inference by performing these density queries
incrementally
. In
Section

4
, we present a second program transformation, which compiles the density program from
Section

3
into an
incremental version
. The incremental version can be run on an
initial
trace
\mathsf{tr}
, to yield a density
\mathsf{w}
and an
updater

\mathsf{u}
. The updater caches certain intermediate values from the computation, and thus helps to calculate the density at a slightly modified trace
\mathsf{tr}^{\prime}
without full recomputation. In many cases, incrementalization delivers asymptotic speedups: in the
\mathsf{gmm}
model, for example, we reduce the cost of some density queries from
O(n)
to
O(1)
, by skipping unnecessary loop iterations.

Concretely, an updater
\mathsf{u}
is applied to a
change description

\mathsf{dtr}
, which precisely encodes the delta from
\mathsf{tr}
to
\mathsf{tr}^{\prime}
. The type of the change description is based on the type of
\mathsf{tr}
(
Fig.

2(d)
). For example,

\displaystyle\mathsf{dtr}

\displaystyle=\{\textnormal{assignments:}\,\{\textnormal{change:}\,[(1,\{\textnormal{new:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.625,0}\mathsf{id}_{64}},\textnormal{same:}\,\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} false}}}\})]\}\}

is one possible change to a trace of
\mathsf{gmm}
, encoding a reassignment of datapoint
1
to cluster
{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0.625,0}\mathsf{id}_{64}}
. Because the entry at the label
assignments
in
\mathsf{tr}
is a list, we supply a list-change, which may specify insertions, deletions, and element modifications; in this case, we only modify element 1 of the list.
Running
\mathsf{u}.\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}(\mathsf{dtr})
efficiently recomputes the density given this change, which only requires re-evaluating a single loop iteration (for the reassigned datapoint).
The updater also returns a new updater
\mathsf{u}^{\prime}
, which can be used to efficiently compute densities for traces similar to
\mathsf{tr}^{\prime}
.

Accelerating Inference.

We can exploit incremental density computation to speed up our inference algorithm of choice.
Figure

2(e)
gives pseudocode for three families of popular Monte Carlo algorithms (Metropolis-Hastings, Gibbs sampling, and sequential Monte Carlo), illustrating the role that incremental densities can play in each. We apply Metropolis-Hastings to our
\mathsf{gmm}
model. The algorithm first constructs an (arbitrary) initial trace whose
data
field is fixed to our observed dataset. It then repeatedly proposes small changes to the trace, accepting or rejecting each change according to the resulting change in density. As
Fig.

2(f)
illustrates, incremental density computation leads to asymptotically faster evaluation of each proposal, and thus dramatically reduces the wall-clock time for inference to converge. Although existing systems, such as Gen
(
13
)
, support incrementality to some extent, our approach exploits some opportunities for asymptotic speedups that existing systems fail to capture. Furthermore, due to the complexity of reasoning about incremental computation and probability, we have found some systems to have incrementalization bugs that can lead inference to silently fail (see
Fig.

2(g)
).
2
2

2

See, e.g.,
https://github.com/probcomp/Gen.jl/issues/512
or
https://github.com/probcomp/Gen.jl/issues/193
.

A key aim of this work is to provide a framework, based on modular program transformations and logical relations, for reasoning clearly about the correctness of incremental densities.

1.2.
Our Approach

Our approach overcomes several key challenges to deliver sound, efficient incremental densities:

(C1) Combining Probabilities and Incrementality.

Existing support for incrementalization in probabilistic programming systems is generally based on a direct consideration of the probabilistic language’s semantics.
Incremental computation in the presence of probabilities is nontrivial: even the existence of program variables can depend on random choices, which complicates the propagation of changes through the program.
As a result, many existing systems rely on complex dependency tracking based on data structures inspired by graphical models
(
31
;
28
;
45
;
13
)
, which can be difficult to reason about (and empirically, is a source of several soundness bugs). Our approach isolates all probabilistic reasoning from incrementalization, by first compiling a probabilistic program to a deterministic density function. Incrementalization is then implemented (and proven correct) for only the deterministic language.

(C2) Incrementalizing Open-Universe Models.

Probabilistic programs often generate latent collections of
objects
whose number is not known in advance (e.g., the clusters in the
\mathsf{gmm}
program). To support efficient recomputation when objects are added and deleted (or, e.g., split and merged), traces and caches must use data structures that make such operations efficient. For example, it is crucial that
\mathsf{gmm}
uses dictionaries with IDs as keys (rather than, e.g., vectors with consecutive integer indices) to store cluster information; otherwise, deleting a latent cluster would require expensive re-indexing to maintain the invariant of consecutive component IDs, inducing
O(n)
changes to cluster assignments. To overcome this challenge, we designed our probabilistic language to support features such as fresh name generation and unordered collections. Using these features, it is possible to express nonparametric, open-universe models in a way that makes them amenable to efficient incrementalization.

(C3) Densities for Open-Universe Models.

Unfortunately, the unique names and unordered collections we use to efficiently encode open-universe models violate two key assumptions behind the density calculations in most PPLs: (1) that all choices of a given type are
either
discrete
or
continuous; and (2) that any given execution trace of a program could have been generated in exactly one way by that program. The first assumption is violated because
names
are sometimes drawn from continuous distributions (to ensure that they will, with probability 1, be fresh
(
40
)
), and sometimes from discrete distributions (when we are choosing randomly to refer to one of a finite set of previously generated IDs). The second assumption is violated because, when an unordered set is stored in a trace, we erase information about the order in which the elements were generated. In
Section

3
, we develop a family of reference measures that allow us to formulate a working notion of density in this setting.
We show how to compute correct densities by including (1) factorial corrections counting the number of ways in which an unordered collection could have been generated, and (2) additional “freshness checks” for names recorded in the trace.

(C4) Representing Changes.

The probabilistic programming system must be able to handle a broad range of user-defined changes to capture trace updates that arise in real-world inference algorithms.
We address this with a type-directed approach: the kinds of changes to a trace depend on the kinds of data in the trace.
First, we use trace typing
(
23
)
to synthesize a rich static type for the traces of a probabilistic program, based on its control flow.
Second, inspired by the incremental
\lambda
-calculus
(
9
;
18
;
37
)
, we define change representations by induction on the trace type. This approach enables concise representations of several common changes that are cumbersome to express (and lead to inefficient incrementalization) in state-of-the-art PPLs.

(C5) Processing Changes Efficiently.

A core technical contribution of this paper is a new approach to typed higher-order incremental computation with caches.
Given a trace change, our goal is to compute a density update with the same asymptotic runtime complexity as an expert manual implementation.
To our knowledge, no existing PPL achieves the best asymptotic performance for updates to nonparametric models such as Dirichlet process mixtures. The key source of high asymptotic complexity is the unnecessary re-execution of loops.
Thus our algorithm is designed to precisely propagate change information about the input trace (
\mathsf{dtr}
) through the density program, so that before entering a loop, we have enough information to compute precisely which loop iterations need to be re-executed. This change propagation is realized by per-primitive change propagators, and rules inspired by the caching incremental
\lambda
-calculus
(
18
)
for composing them. We develop a new approach to implementing this design in a typed, higher-order setting, which allows us to view loops themselves as combinator-like, higher-order primitives with their own built-in logic for caching, change propagation, and incrementalization.

(C6) Reasoning about Correctness.

Our two-stage approach allows us to reason separately about the correctness of density computation and incrementalization; we prove each transformation correct using independent logical relations arguments over the denotational semantics of our languages. Interestingly, previous work on cache-based incrementalization
(
18
)
was formalized only in an untyped setting, because the types of caches of intermediate values quickly become unwieldy. We address this challenge by encapsulating caches in an opaque updater type
\mathsf{Upd}(\tau;\tau^{\prime})
, which hides the cache type after construction.
The semantics treats updaters as coinductive data types (infinite trees of all possible future update sequences) and can ignore caching concerns. This enables a modular logical relations proof over a standard, set-theoretic denotational semantics.

Contributions.

In summary, we make the following contributions.

•

A new approach to typed higher-order incremental computation (
Section

4
):

We present a new program transformation for turning higher-order deterministic programs into
updaters
that efficiently compute changes to their outputs given changes to their inputs.
Our updaters support caches of intermediate results that are encapsulated via coinductive
updater types
, giving the first typed treatment of caching incremental computation while staying within the simply-typed setting.
Each update also returns a new updater to process subsequent changes, which is essential for iterative applications such as Markov chain Monte Carlo inference.

•

Incremental densities for open-universe models (
Sections

2
and
3
):
We present a program transformation that compiles probabilistic programs into deterministic, incrementalizable density functions. These densities are defined on typed execution traces, based on a novel extension of
trace typing

(
23
;
5
;
48
;
24
)
to support unordered collections and named objects. In combination with our incrementalization transformation, this enables efficient trace updates when inferring unknown collections of entities in open-universe and nonparametric models
(
31
;
33
)
.

•

Denotational correctness arguments (
Sections

3.3
and
4.3
):

We present a denotational account of the correctness of both stages of our pipeline.
For the density transformation, we develop novel semantic techniques for reasoning about the densities of probabilistic programs that may generate unordered collections of names.
For the incrementalization transformation, we present the first denotational argument for the correctness of caching incremental computation, which relies on a new coinduction-based reasoning principle for the correctness of updaters.

•

Empirical validation (
Section

5
):

We implement our approach for an embedded probabilistic DSL in Julia and compare it with Gen
(
13
)
, a state-of-the-art programmable inference system implemented in the same host language.
We show that our approach offers significant speedups compared to naive density evaluation and its asymptotic runtime is the same or better than Gen.

2.
Probabilistic and Deterministic Languages

This section introduces the syntax and semantics of three related programming languages. As illustrated in
Fig.

1
, the transformations we develop in
Sections

3
and
4
translate between these languages: user probabilistic programs in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
are translated to deterministic density functions in
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
, which are further translated into incremental density functions in
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
. The grammars and typing rules for all three languages are presented in
Figs.

3
and
4
.

2.1.
Probabilistic Language

Our probabilistic language,
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
, is the user-facing language for encoding probabilistic models.

Ground Types.

\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
is a variant of the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus with the following ground types:

\sigma::=\mathbb{B}\mid\mathbb{R}\mid\mathbb{N}\mid\mathsf{Name}\mid\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}\mid\mathsf{List}\;\sigma\mid\mathsf{NameMap}\;\sigma\mid\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\sigma_{n}\}\quad\quad(\ell_{i}\in\mathcal{L})

Each type
\sigma
is taken to denote a space of values
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
, and a term
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
denotes a map
\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!:\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
.
3
3

3

As is standard in the semantics of higher-order probabilistic programming languages, these spaces are
quasi-Borel spaces

(
21
)
, a drop-in replacement for measurable spaces with better support for function types.
The types
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathbb{R}
denote the standard spaces of Booleans, natural numbers, and reals respectively. To model
names
, we take
\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!=[0,1]
, exploiting an insight of
40
that if names are interpreted as real numbers, fresh name generation can be modeled as continuous sampling (as, with probability 1, two independent samples will not be equal). Record types
\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}
, with field labels drawn from a countably infinite set
\mathcal{L}
, denote indexed products, with projections
\pi_{\ell_{1}},\dots,\pi_{\ell_{n}}
. The empty record
\{\}
serves as a unit type for
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
.
\mathsf{List}\;\sigma
denotes
\sqcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!^{i}
. The type
\mathsf{NameMap}\;\sigma
denotes finite dictionaries with keys in
\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!
and values in
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
, which we represent concretely as
sorted
lists of
\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
pairs without duplicate keys (sorted by the usual order on
\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!=[0,1]
). We write
\mathsf{NameSet}
as sugar for
\mathsf{NameMap}\;\{\}
. We use the syntactic sugar
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} if}}}\;s\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} then}}}\;t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} else}}}\;t^{\prime}
for a primitive operation
\mathsf{ite}_{\sigma}(s,t,t^{\prime})
where
t
and
t^{\prime}
have ground type
\sigma
, and
s[t]
for a primitive
\mathsf{get}_{\sigma}(t,s)
that indexes lists and maps.

Function Types.

Function types in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
are written
\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau
. The annotation
\kappa
on the arrow specifies the type of data in a closure’s captured environment, but does not affect the semantics:
\left\llbracket\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau\right\rrbracket\!=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\Rightarrow\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
(where
\Rightarrow
constructs the semantic function space). For example, we have

y:\mathbb{R},z:\mathbb{N}\vdash\lambda x.~x<y:\mathbb{R}\to_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{B}

because the function
\lambda x.~x<y
closes over the variable
y
of type
\mathbb{R}
.
4
4

4

Technically, the typing rules compute the equivalent type
\mathbb{R}\to_{\{\}{}\times\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{B}
.
(It is admittedly unusual to track this information in the type of a function. More details will be provided in
Section

4
, but the intuition is that our incrementalization transformation will represent
changes
to functions of type
\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau
as changes to the data they close over, and so the type
\kappa
of this data must be explicit.)

\displaystyle\text{Types }\sigma,\tau

\displaystyle::=\mathbb{B}\mid\mathbb{N}\mid\mathbb{R}\mid\mathsf{Name}\mid\sigma\times\tau\mid\mathsf{List}\;\sigma\mid\mathsf{NameMap}\;\sigma\mid\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\mid\underbrace{\hbox{\pagecolor{genlangcolor!50}$\mathsf{Dist}_{\kappa}\;\tau$}\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{genlangcolor!50}$\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\tau\mid\tau\to_{\sigma}\tau^{\prime}$}}_{\hbox{\pagecolor{genlangcolor!50}$\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}$}}\mid\underbrace{\hbox{\pagecolor{inclangcolor!50}$\tau\to_{\sigma}\tau^{\prime}$}}_{\hbox{\pagecolor{inclangcolor!50}$\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}$}}\mid\underbrace{\hbox{\pagecolor{corelangcolor!50}$\tau\to\tau^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{Sub}\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}\mid\mathsf{Upd}(\tau;\tau^{\prime})$}}_{\hbox{\pagecolor{corelangcolor!50}$\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}$}}

\displaystyle\text{Terms: }s,t

\displaystyle::=x\mid c\mid x:=s;t\mid\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp t\mid s\;t\mid(s,t)\mid t.1\mid t.2

in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
:

\displaystyle\qquad\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{genlangcolor!50}$\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;t\mid x\leftarrow s;t\mid\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t\mid t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} @}}}\;\ell$}

\displaystyle\qquad\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{genlangcolor!50}$\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Set}}}\;\mathit{xs}\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\;(v_{1}:=y_{1}[u_{1}],\dots,v_{n}:=y_{n}[u_{n}])\;\{\;t\;\}$}

\displaystyle\qquad\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{genlangcolor!50}$\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Range}}}\;w\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} with}}}\;z:=s\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\;(v_{1}:=y_{1}[u_{1}],\dots,v_{n}:=y_{n}[u_{n}])\;\{\;t\;\}$}

in
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
:

\displaystyle\qquad\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{inclangcolor!50}$t.\ell$}\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{inclangcolor!50}$t|_{\{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}\}}$}\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{inclangcolor!50}$\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\;\mathit{xs}\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\;(v_{1}:=y_{1}[u_{1}],\dots,v_{n}:=y_{n}[u_{n}])\;\{\;t\;\}$}

\displaystyle\qquad\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{inclangcolor!50}$\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\;\mathit{xs}\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} with}}}\;z:=s\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\;(v_{1}:=y_{1}[u_{1}],\dots,v_{n}:=y_{n}[u_{n}])\;\{\;t\;\}$}

in
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
:

\displaystyle\qquad\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{corelangcolor!50}$t.\ell$}\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{corelangcolor!50}$t|_{\{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}\}}$}\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{corelangcolor!50}$\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} has}}}_{\ell}\;t$}\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{corelangcolor!50}$\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} mkUpd}}}(s;t)$}\mid\hbox{\pagecolor{corelangcolor!50}$s.\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}(t)$}

Figure 3
.
Syntax of our languages
\hbox{\pagecolor{genlangcolor!50}$\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}$},\hbox{\pagecolor{inclangcolor!50}$\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}$},\hbox{\pagecolor{corelangcolor!50}$\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}$}
. Labels
\ell
are from a countable set
\mathcal{L}
.

Probabilistic Programs.

Probabilistic programs in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
have types of the form
\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\tau
:
\tau
is the return type of the probabilistic program, and
\sigma
is its
trace type
, i.e., the data type used to represent reified traces of its execution
(
23
)
.
As with function types,
\kappa
is an environment annotation, reflecting the types of any free variables captured by the probabilistic computation. (We omit it when none are captured.) For example, the generative model of
Fig.

2(b)
has type
\mathsf{P}\,\sigma\,(\mathsf{List}\;\mathbb{R}^{2})
, where

\displaystyle\sigma=\{

\displaystyle\textnormal{clusterNames}:\mathsf{NameSet},\textnormal{params}:\mathsf{NameMap}\;\{\textnormal{mean}:\mathbb{R}^{2},\textnormal{cov}:\mathbb{R}^{4}\},

\displaystyle\textnormal{weights}:\mathsf{NameMap}\;\mathbb{R},\textnormal{assignments}:\mathsf{List}\;\mathsf{Name},\textnormal{data}:\mathsf{List}\;\mathbb{R}^{2}\}

Semantically,
\left\llbracket\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\tau\right\rrbracket\!=\mathsf{Prob}~\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\times(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\Rightarrow\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!)
. That is, a probabilistic program denotes a pair
(\mu,f)
, where
\mu\in\mathsf{Prob}~\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
is a distribution on traces, and
f:\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
is a deterministic function mapping traces to corresponding return values. Formally,
\mathsf{Prob}~X
is a subset of the function space
(X\Rightarrow[0,\infty])\Rightarrow[0,\infty]
: we identify a probability distribution
\mu
with its
expectation operator
, which sends functions
f:X\to[0,\infty]
to their expected values
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f]
. For example, the uniform distribution on
[0,1]
is
\Lambda_{[0,1]}=f\mapsto\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\mathrm{d}x
, and the
Dirac delta
distribution at a point
x
is
\delta_{x}=f\mapsto f(x)
.
For
k:X\to\mathsf{Prob}~Y
, we write
\mu;k\in\mathsf{Prob}~Y
for the composition
f\mapsto\mu(x\mapsto k(x)(f))
. We write
x\leftsquigarrow\mu;\nu
, where
x
occurs free in
\nu
, to mean
\mu;(x\mapsto\nu)
.

Probabilistic programs are built by composing
primitive distributions
of type
\mathsf{Dist}_{\kappa}\;\sigma
, where
\left\llbracket\mathsf{Dist}_{\kappa}\;\sigma\right\rrbracket\!=\mathsf{Prob}~\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
. The key constructs are:

(1)

Deterministic computation.
A probabilistic program can return a deterministically computed value using the construct
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;t
. The execution traces of deterministic programs are empty, because there are no random choices to record. For example, we have the judgments

\displaystyle\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;(5+3,\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} true}}}):\mathsf{P}\,\{\}\,(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle x:\mathbb{R}\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;x<3:\mathsf{P}_{\mathbb{R}}\,\{\}\,\mathbb{B}

where on the right, the environment annotation
\kappa=\mathbb{R}
indicates that the program closes over a variable of type
\mathbb{R}
. For
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
and
\gamma\in\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\!
, we have
\left\llbracket\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)=(\delta_{\{\}},\_\mapsto\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma))
.

(2)

Sampling primitive distributions.
The construct
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t
generates and returns a sample from the primitive distribution specified by
t:\mathsf{Dist}\;\sigma
. For example,
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;\mathsf{normal}(0,1):\mathsf{P}\,\mathbb{R}\,\mathbb{R}
samples a standard Gaussian and returns it. The trace simply records the sampled value:

\left\llbracket\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)=(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma),\mathrm{id})

In addition to the standard discrete and continuous primitive distributions,
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
has a primitive
\mathsf{fresh}:\mathsf{Dist}\;\mathbb{N}\to\mathsf{Dist}\;\mathsf{NameSet}
. This primitive generates a random number
n
from its argument distribution,
5
5

5

When we wish to generate a fixed number of fresh names, we write
\mathsf{fresh}(n)
as sugar for
\mathsf{fresh}(\mathsf{dirac}(n))
.
then returns a set of
n
freshly generated names (i.e.,
n
independent samples from the uniform distribution on
[0,1]
).

(3)

Labeling random choices.
The construct
t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} @}}}\;\ell

labels
the random choice(s) made by
t
with
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
; the trace type of
t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} @}}}\;\ell
is the singleton record
\{\ell:\sigma\}
, where
\sigma
is the trace type of
t
. For example, we have
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;\mathsf{normal}(0,1)\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} @}}}\;\textnormal{val}:\mathsf{P}\,\{\textnormal{val}:\mathbb{R}\}\,\mathbb{R}
. Semantically,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} @}}}\;\ell\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)=(x\leftsquigarrow\mu;\delta_{\{\ell\mapsto x\}},f\circ\pi_{\ell})

\displaystyle\text{where }(\mu,f):=\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

(4)

Sequencing.
The construct
x\leftarrow t;s
runs
t
and binds its return value to
x
, then runs
s
. The terms
t
and
s
must both be probabilistic programs with disjoint record trace types; the trace type of
x\leftarrow t;s
is the merge of the two record types. We write
x\sim t
as syntactic sugar for
x\leftarrow t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} @}}}\;x
(or for
x\leftarrow\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} @}}}\;x
if
t:\mathsf{Dist}\;\sigma
). For example, we have the judgment

x:\mathbb{R}\vdash y\sim\mathsf{normal}(x,1);z\sim{\mathsf{beta}(1,2)};\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;z<y:\mathsf{P}_{\mathbb{R}}\,\{y:\mathbb{R},z:\mathbb{R}\}\,\mathbb{B}

where the trace type
\{y:\mathbb{R},z:\mathbb{R}\}
is the concatenation of
\{y:\mathbb{R}\}
,
\{z:\mathbb{R}\}
, and
\{\}
.
Semantically,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket x\leftarrow t;s\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)=(\rho_{1}\leftsquigarrow\mu;\rho_{2}\leftsquigarrow k(f(\rho_{1}));\delta_{\rho_{1}+\!\!+\rho_{2}},\rho\mapsto g(f(\rho|_{L_{1}}))(\rho|_{L_{2}}))

where
(\mu,f)=\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)
,
\langle k,g\rangle=\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma[x\mapsto-])
, and
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are the label sets of the trace types of
t
and
s
.
Our probabilistic program types form a
graded monad
with grade
\sigma
.
6
6

6

However, trace types
\sigma
form only a
partial
monoid, because only disjoint record types
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
can be merged.

(5)

Loops.
The loop
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Range}}}\;d\;\{\;t\;\}
, where
d:\mathsf{Dist}\;\mathbb{N}
, draws
n\sim d
, then iterates from
x:=1
to
n
.
7
7

7

We write
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Range}}}\;n\;\{\;t\;\}
, where
n:\mathbb{N}
, as shorthand for
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Range}}}\;\mathsf{dirac}(n)\;\{\;t\;\}
.
Its traces are lists, where each element is a trace of
t
, and
n
is implicitly recorded as the length of the list. Its return value is also a list, of the values returned by
t
at each iteration. Optionally, an accumulator may be initialized and updated each iteration:

\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;\mathsf{i}\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Range}}}\;5\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} with}}}\;\mathsf{z}:=0\;\{\;\mathsf{z}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{z}+1;\mathsf{y}\sim\mathsf{normal}(\mathsf{z}^{\prime},0.1);\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z}^{\prime})\;\}:\mathsf{P}\,(\mathsf{List}\;\{\textnormal{y}:\mathbb{R}\})\,(\mathsf{List}\;\mathbb{R})

The construct
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Set}}}\;d\;\{\;t\;\}
loops over an unordered set of names (no accumulator). This loop draws
\mathit{names}\sim d
, and runs
t
(of type
\mathsf{P}\,\sigma\,\tau
) once for each name, collecting the results into a
\mathsf{NameMap}\;\tau
. Its trace type is
\mathsf{NameMap}\;\sigma
, as its traces store a trace of
t
for each iteration.
The clause
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\;(v_{1}:=y_{1}[u_{1}];\dots;v_{n}:=y_{n}[u_{n}])
may also be added before a loop’s body. This is equivalent to adding
v_{1}:=y_{1}[u_{1}];\dots;v_{n}:=y_{n}[u_{n}];
to the loop body, but tells the system to track the indices of the collections
y_{i}
accessed at each iteration for finer-grained incrementality.

\frac{}{\mathsf{CtxType}(\bullet)=\{\}}\par\frac{\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma)=\tau}{\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma,x:\tau^{\prime})=\tau\times\tau^{\prime}}\par\frac{\Gamma,x:\tau\vdash t:\mathsf{P}_{\kappa^{\prime}}\,\sigma\,(\tau\times\rho)}{\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} 8.50012pt\textsc{right={\scriptsize(\hbox{\pagecolor{genlangcolor!50}$\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}$})}}$}\par\resizebox{22609920}{}{$\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{xs}:\mathsf{Dist}_{\kappa}\;\mathsf{NameSet}\hskip 2.2.
Deterministic Languages

In addition to
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
, we have two deterministic languages:
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
(the
source language
of our incrementalization transformation) and
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
(the
target language
of our incrementalization transformation). Both
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
and
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
have set-theoretic denotational semantics (
Figs.

5
and
6
): each type
\tau
denotes a set
\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
, and each term
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
denotes a function
\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!:\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
. The deterministic languages inherit the ground types of
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
.

The
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
Calculus.

As in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
,
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
function types
\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau
are annotated with captured environment types
\kappa
. But in
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
, the
semantics
of a function tracks how it depends on its closed-over data:

\left\llbracket{\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau}\right\rrbracket\!=\left\llbracket\kappa\right\rrbracket\!\times(\left\llbracket\kappa\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\Rightarrow\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!)

The semantics of function abstraction and application explicitly manipulate these closures.

The
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
language also features a
deterministic
looping construct,
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\;\mathit{xs}\;\{\;t\;\}
, where
\mathit{xs}
may either be of type
\mathsf{List}\;\sigma
(in which case
x:\sigma\vdash t:\tau
and the loop returns a
\mathsf{List}\;\tau
) or of type
\mathsf{NameSet}
(in which case
x:\mathsf{Name}\vdash t:\tau
and the loop returns a
\mathsf{NameMap}\;\tau
).
As in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
, the loop supports a
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\;(v_{1}:=y_{1}[u_{1}],\dots,v_{n}:=y_{n}[u_{n}])
clause for fine-grained tracking of the indices at which each iteration accesses the collections
y_{i}
.
Iterating over lists (but not unordered collections) also supports a variant with an accumulator, introduced by the clause
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} with}}}~z:=s
before the loop body.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathbb{B}\right\rrbracket\!=\{0,1\}\hskip 8.50012pt\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!=[0,1]\hskip 8.50012pt\textstyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{List}\;\sigma\right\rrbracket\!=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!^{*}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!^{n}\hskip 8.50012pt\left\llbracket\mathsf{NameMap}\;\tau\right\rrbracket\!=\{f:A\to\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!\mid A\subset_{\mathsf{fin}}\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!\}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\tau\to_{\kappa}\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!=\left\llbracket\kappa\right\rrbracket\!\times(\left\llbracket\kappa\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!\Rightarrow\left\llbracket\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!)\hskip 17.00024pt\textstyle\left\llbracket\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}\right\rrbracket\!=\{r:\{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}\}\to\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\left\llbracket\tau_{i}\right\rrbracket\!\mid r(\ell_{i})\in\left\llbracket\tau_{i}\right\rrbracket\!\}

\displaystyle=\left((\gamma[x_{1}],\dots,\gamma[x_{n}]),((v_{1},\dots,v_{n}),v)\mapsto\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma[x_{1}\mapsto v_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mapsto v_{n},x\mapsto v])\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\text{ where }\Gamma|_{\mathsf{FV}(\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp t)}=x_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\tau_{n}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket s\;t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle={f(\mathit{env},\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma))\text{ where }\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)=(\mathit{env},f)}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\scriptsize\begin{aligned} &\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\;\mathit{xs}\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} with}}}\;z:=s\\
&\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\;(v_{1}:=y_{1}[u_{1}],\\

.
Semantics of
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
(selected rules).

The
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
Calculus.

The semantics of
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
has standard function types (without environment annotations
\kappa
).
In addition to the ground types of
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
, it has
subrecord types

\mathsf{Sub}\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\sigma_{n}\}
. Values of a subrecord may assign data to only a subset of the record’s labels.
When accessing a label that is not present in a subrecord, we return a default value
\mathsf{default}_{\tau}\in\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
, chosen by induction on
\tau
(e.g.,
0
for
\mathbb{N}
, the empty map for
\mathsf{NameMap}\;\tau
,
x\mapsto\mathsf{default}_{\tau^{\prime}}
for functions
\tau\to\tau^{\prime}
etc.).
The core language also has an
updater
type
\mathsf{Upd}(\tau;\tau^{\prime})
. Updaters are constructed using
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} mkUpd}}}(s;t)
and applied using
s.\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}(t)
.
We defer the discussion of these constructs to
Section

4
.

3.
Density Transformation

The first step in our pipeline is to translate a probabilistic
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
program into a deterministic
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
program that computes its trace distribution’s
density function
. This isolates all probabilistic reasoning from incrementalization, which we discuss in
Section

4
.

3.1.
Densities of Traced Programs with Name Generation

Given a program of type
\mathsf{P}\,\sigma\,\tau
, we wish to compute its
density function
of type
\sigma\to\mathbb{R}
. This function maps an execution trace, specifying values for all random choices, to a nonnegative real number, which quantifies the likelihood of the given trace. A trace is likely if the individual random choices are; the overall density is a product of the densities of each recorded primitive choice. To make these ideas precise, we require the following definition from measure theory.

Definition 3.1 (Radon-Nikodym derivative).

Let
\mu
be a probability distribution on a space
X
, and
\nu
a measure on
X
, called the
reference measure
. A function
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu}:X\to\mathbb{R}
is called a
density
or
Radon-Nikodym derivative
of
\mu
with respect to
\nu
if for all
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f]=\int f(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu}(x)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)
.

Here,
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f]
denotes the expected value of
f
with inputs sampled from
\mu
. Radon-Nikodym derivatives generalize the standard notions of
probability mass function
and
probability density function
: when
\nu
is the counting measure
\#_{X}
on a set
X
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu}
is
\mu
’s mass function,
and when
\nu
is the Lebesgue measure
\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}
, it is
\mu
’s density function.
Probabilistic programs generally make both discrete and continuous choices, so their traces are typically heterogeneous products. The standard approach to thinking about densities of such programs is to use inductively defined, type-indexed reference measures
\nu_{\sigma}
, designed so that the overall density will be a product of the mass functions of all discrete choices and the density functions of all continuous choices:

\displaystyle\nu_{\mathbb{R}}=\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}\quad\quad\nu_{\mathbb{B}}=\#_{\mathbb{B}}\quad\quad\nu_{\mathbb{N}}=\#_{\mathbb{N}}\quad\quad\nu_{\sigma\times\tau}=\nu_{\sigma}\otimes\nu_{\tau}\quad\quad\dots

Our setting, however, is complicated by the presence of
names
. Semantically,
\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!
is the real interval
[0,1]
. However, whether a randomly chosen name is a
continuous
or a
discrete
choice (i.e., whether we should use a
density
or
mass
function for its likelihood) varies from program to program. For example, consider the program in
Fig.

7
, which generates several fresh names and later chooses one of them uniformly at random. The fresh names (at address
opts
) are continuous samples, but the selected name (at address
chosen
) is a discrete choice.
Thus, the trace density should have as factors both the
density
of the
\mathsf{fresh}
primitive,
8
8

8

The distribution over sets of
n
fresh names is the uniform distribution on
\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in[0,1]^{n}\mid x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}\}
, a set with volume
\frac{1}{n!}
. Thus the density at any point within this set is
1/\frac{1}{n!}=n!
, and the density at other points is
0
.
and the
mass
of the
\mathsf{unif}
primitive.

\begin{aligned} &\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} def}}}\ \mathsf{program}:=\\
&\quad\mathsf{n}\sim\mathsf{poisson}(3);\\
&\quad\mathsf{opts}\sim\mathsf{fresh}(\mathsf{n}+1);\\
&\\
&\quad\mathsf{xs}\sim\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\ \_\ \textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} inSet}}}\ \mathsf{opts}\ \{\\
&\quad\quad\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;\mathsf{normal}(0,1)\\
&\quad\};\\
&\quad\mathsf{chosen}\sim\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{opts});\\
&\quad{\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;\mathsf{xs}[\mathsf{chosen}]}\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} def}}}\ \mathsf{density}:=\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}(\mathsf{t},\mathsf{U})\ldotp\\
&\quad\mathsf{w_{1}}:=\mathsf{poissonD}(3)(\mathsf{t}.\textnormal{n},\mathsf{U});\\
&\quad\mathsf{w_{2}}:=\mathsf{freshD}(\mathsf{t}.\textnormal{n}+1)(\mathsf{t}.\textnormal{opts},\mathsf{U});\\
&\quad\mathsf{U}:=\mathsf{U}\cup\mathsf{t}.\textnormal{opts};\\
&\quad\mathsf{w_{3}}:=\mathsf{product}(\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\ \mathsf{o}\ \textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\ \mathsf{t}.\textnormal{opts}\ \textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\ (\mathsf{x}:=\mathsf{t}.\textnormal{xs}[\mathsf{o}])\ \{\\
&\quad\quad\mathsf{normalD}(0,1)(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{U})\\
&\quad\});\\
&\quad\mathsf{w_{4}}:=\mathsf{unifD}(\mathsf{t}.\textnormal{opts})(\mathsf{t}.\textnormal{chosen},\mathsf{U});\\
&\quad{(\mathsf{t}.\textnormal{xs}[\mathsf{t}.\textnormal{chosen}],\,\mathsf{w_{1}}\cdot\mathsf{w_{2}}\cdot\mathsf{w_{3}}\cdot\mathsf{w_{4}})}\end{aligned}

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{\emptyset}^{\sigma}}(t)=\mathsf{Pois}(t.\textnormal{n};3)\cdot\underbrace{(t.\textnormal{n}+1)!\cdot\mathbf{1}\!\left[|t.\textnormal{opts}|=t.\textnormal{n}+1\right]}_{\mathsf{freshD}(t.\textnormal{n}+1)(t.\textnormal{opts},\emptyset)}\cdot\prod_{o\in t.\textnormal{opts}}\mathcal{N}(t.\textnormal{xs}[o];0,1)\cdot\underbrace{\frac{\mathbf{1}[t.\textnormal{chosen}\in t.\textnormal{opts}]}{t.\textnormal{n}+1}}_{\mathsf{unifD}(t.\textnormal{opts})(t.\textnormal{chosen},t.\textnormal{opts})}

Figure 7
.
Computing the density of a probabilistic program.
In this figure,
\mathsf{distD}
denotes the density function of a distribution
\mathsf{dist}
, and
\sigma
(used in
\nu_{\emptyset}^{\sigma}
) is the trace type of
\mathsf{program}
.

Our inductively defined reference measures, in
Fig.

8
, formalize this intuition. The reference measures are indexed by both a type
\sigma
and a
support

U
, which is a countable subset of
\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!
. The reference measure
\nu^{\mathsf{Name}}_{U}
is a
sum
of
\Lambda_{[0,1]}
and
\#_{U}
, indicating that randomly chosen names may either be continuous choices (i.e., freshly generated), or discrete choices from the support set
U
of available names.
The reference measure for product types makes the names that appear in the first component (
\mathsf{names}(a)
) available as new atoms for discrete choices in the second component.
9
9

9

The order of products is arbitrary, in that for all ground types
\sigma
,
\tau
, we have
\nu_{U}^{\sigma\times\tau}=\mathsf{swap}_{*}\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\sigma}
(proven in
Section

B.1
).
These definitions give us a sufficiently general notion of
density
to handle all programs in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
:

Lemma 3.2.

Let
\vdash t:\mathsf{P}\,\sigma\,\tau
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
. Then
\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!_{1}
has a density
\frac{\mathrm{d}\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{\emptyset}^{\sigma}}:\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\to\mathbb{R}
with respect to
\nu_{\emptyset}^{\sigma}
.

\nu_{U}^{\sigma}

=

\#_{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!}\text{ for }\sigma\in\{\{\},\mathbb{B},\mathbb{N}\}

\nu_{U}^{\mathbb{R}}

=

\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}

\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}

=

\#_{U}+\Lambda_{[0,1]}

\nu_{U}^{\sigma\times\tau}

=

a\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\sigma};b\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U\cup\mathsf{names}(a)}^{\tau};\mathsf{ret}~(a,b)

\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{List}\;\sigma}

=

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\nu_{U}^{\sigma^{n}}

\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{NameMap}\;\sigma}

=

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\nu_{U}^{(\mathsf{Name}\times\sigma)^{n}}

.
Indexed families of reference measures, defined inductively on the ground types of

.

3.2.
Density Transformation

We now present a program transformation,
\mathcal{W}\!\left\{-\right\}
, which mechanically translates probabilistic programs into deterministic ones computing their density functions. More precisely, we translate a program of type
\mathsf{P}\,\sigma\,\tau
into a deterministic program with two inputs and two outputs, as illustrated in
Fig.

7
. The inputs are a
trace

\mathsf{t}
and a set of previously generated names
\mathsf{U}
. The outputs are a return value—the value returned by the program when its random choices are fixed to those recorded in
\mathsf{t}
—and a nonnegative real, the density of the program with respect to
\nu_{\mathsf{U}}^{\sigma}
at the trace
\mathsf{t}
.

Intuitively, the density program works by executing the same logic as the original probabilistic program. When a primitive random choice is encountered, we look up the corresponding value in the trace. We use the primitive distribution’s built-in density (passing in the choice’s value and the current support set
\mathsf{U}
of names) to compute its likelihood. When we find new names in the trace, we add them to
\mathsf{U}
.
10
10

10

Tracking the support
\mathsf{U}
allows the density function to return
0
when multiple purportedly fresh names in a trace are identical. In particular, the built-in density
\mathsf{freshD}(n)(\mathsf{names},\mathsf{U})
is either
n!
(if
|\mathsf{names}|=n
and
\mathsf{names}\cap\mathsf{U}=\emptyset
), or
0
otherwise. This logic is necessary for the density to be technically correct, but properly implemented inference algorithms should never consider traces that trigger freshness violations. As such, much like array bounds checking, support tracking is a feature that can be turned off for performance after the user is confident that inference is correctly implemented.
Then we compute the return value and the product of all primitive densities.

This algorithm is formalized as a program transformation in
Figs.

9
and
10
. Given an open term
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
, we translate it to a term
\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\Gamma\right\}\vdash\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}:\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\tau\right\}
in the target language,
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
. Because the density function largely follows the same logic as the original probabilistic program,
\mathcal{W}\!\left\{-\right\}
is defined to “pass through” many constructs, sending tuples to tuples, projections to projections, abstractions to abstractions, and applications to applications (preserving the environment annotations
\kappa
on higher types). The interesting cases are how it handles probabilistic program terms. Deterministic programs
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;t
have constant density
1
at the empty trace. The density of a program that makes a single primitive random sample,
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t
, is just the density of the primitive distribution
t
. Compound programs
x\leftarrow t_{1};t_{2}
are handled by separately computing the density of
t_{1}
(on the subtrace containing the choices made by
t_{1}
) and the density of
t_{2}
(on the subtrace containing the choices made by
t_{2}
), then taking their product. (Any new names appearing in the trace of
t_{1}
are also unioned into the running support set
\mathsf{U}
, which is passed to the density for
t_{2}
. This reflects that fresh names generated by
t_{1}
may occur non-fresh, as discrete choices, in
t_{2}
.)

Note that loops in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
are translated into more elaborate loops in
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
. The target-language loops iterate over a collection of subtraces, computing the density of each subtrace under the loop body while maintaining a growing set of used names
V
. These densities are then multiplied together—along with the density of the distribution used to decide how many iterations to run, if this was a random choice—to yield a density for the overall loop.

\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}

\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\sigma\times\tau\right\}

=

=

\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}\times\mathsf{NameSet}\to_{\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\kappa\right\}}\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\times\mathbb{R}

3.3.
Correctness

The density transformation is correct in the following sense:

Theorem 3.3.

Let
t
be a closed
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
term of type
\mathsf{P}\,\sigma\,\tau
, with trace distribution
\mu=\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!_{1}
. Then for all traces
\rho\in\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{\emptyset}^{\sigma}}(\rho)=\left\llbracket(\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}(x,\emptyset)).2\right\rrbracket\!(x\mapsto\rho)
.

That is, running the density program on a trace
\rho
and the empty name set
\emptyset
, then extracting the second component of the output, does yield the correct density of the program’s trace distribution, evaluated at
\rho
. This result is established by a logical relations argument, developed in
Appendix

B
.

4.
Incrementalization

We now describe a program transformation
\mathcal{I}\!\left\{-\right\}
for incrementalizing a deterministic program. The incremental version of a function
f:\sigma\to\tau
(on ground types
\sigma
and
\tau
) has type

f^{\prime}:\sigma\to\tau\times\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\sigma);\Delta(\tau))

Like
f
, it accepts an argument of type
\sigma
, and computes an initial output of type
\tau
.
Additionally, it returns an
updater
, which encapsulates a cache of intermediate results and can thus efficiently compute output changes given changes to the input.
Concretely, let
(y_{1},u_{1}):=f^{\prime}(x_{1})
and
\mathit{dx}_{1}
an input change from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
.
Then
u_{1}.\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}(\mathit{dx}_{1})=:(\mathit{dy}_{1},u_{2})
yields an output change
\mathit{dy}_{1}
from
y_{1}:=f(x_{1})
to
y_{2}:=f(x_{2})
, and a new updater
u_{2}
for changes on top of
x_{2}
.
This allows us the flexibility to freely stack input changes and compute the corresponding output changes.

The key idea of the transformation is that each primitive operation (e.g., arithmetic operations and list/map operations) has an incremental version as above.
Our program transformation composes these incremental primitives and wires all the change information and updaters together.
Even if a given primitive does not benefit from incrementalization itself, it is useful to propagate information about its output changes to downstream computations.
This is most valuable for loops, enabling us to rerun as few loop iterations as possible, which can yield asymptotic speedups.

4.1.
Change Types and Updaters

We associate every type
\tau
with a change type
\Delta(\tau)
, which represents changes to values of type
\tau
(
Fig.

11
).
For basic types like
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{B}
, a change is simply a new value together with a flag indicating whether the value has changed.
For product types, we make a distinction between pairs and records: a change to a pair is a pair of changes to the components, whereas a change to a record is a subrecord of changes to the fields (omitting unchanged fields).
Changes to collections (lists and maps) store changes to the elements at specific indices or keys and insertions/deletions of elements.
For closures, we do not allow the function itself (“its code”) to change, only its captured environment, to keep updates efficient.
Finally, changes to contexts are simply changes to the variables in the context.
The change types are designed to strike a balance between keeping change representations small and including enough information to avoid caching.
For instance, changes to atomic ground types include the new value, so operations do not need to cache their arguments.

Updaters.

Updaters encapsulate cached intermediate values and the logic for using them to efficiently recompute an output.
We define
\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\tau);\Delta(\tau^{\prime}))
as an
opaque
type with a constructor
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} mkUpd}}}(\mathsf{cache};\mathsf{update})
accepting an initial
\mathsf{cache}:\sigma
and a function
\mathsf{update}:\Delta(\tau)\times\sigma\to\Delta(\tau^{\prime})\times\sigma
, which takes an input change and a cache and returns an output change and a new cache.
Note that updater types hide the cache type
\sigma
after construction, like existential types.
This is an innovation over previous work, and enables type-directed reasoning about updaters.

To use an updater
\mathsf{u}
, one applies it to a change
\mathsf{dx}
, written
\mathsf{u}.\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}(\mathsf{dx})=:(\mathsf{dy},\mathsf{u}^{\prime})
, which yields an output change
\mathsf{dy}
and a new updater
\mathsf{u}^{\prime}
that can be used to compute output changes on top of
\mathsf{dx}
.
Updater construction and application satisfy the following program equivalence:

\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} mkUpd}}}(\mathsf{cache};\mathsf{upd}).\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}(\mathsf{dx})=\{(\mathsf{dy},\mathsf{cache}^{\prime}):=\mathsf{upd}\;(\mathsf{dx},\mathsf{cache});(\mathsf{dy},\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} mkUpd}}}(\mathsf{cache}^{\prime};\mathsf{upd}))\}

Semantics of Updaters.

Semantically, updaters are treated as coinductive data types:

\text{\emph{Intuition: }}\quad\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} codata}}}\;\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\tau);\Delta(\tau^{\prime}))\approx\Delta(\tau)\to\Delta(\tau^{\prime})\times\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\tau);\Delta(\tau^{\prime}))

Thus its denotation is an infinite-depth tree whose edges are labeled by values of
\Delta(\tau)
and nodes by values of
\Delta(\tau^{\prime})
(see
Fig.

6
).
(Category theoretically, this is the final coalgebra of the functor
F(X)=\left\llbracket\Delta(\tau)\right\rrbracket\!\Rightarrow\left\llbracket\Delta(\tau^{\prime})\right\rrbracket\!\times X
.)
Formally, we represent such trees as functions
\left\llbracket\Delta(\tau)\right\rrbracket\!^{+}\to\left\llbracket\Delta(\tau^{\prime})\right\rrbracket\!
from non-empty lists of
\Delta(\tau)
(representing the path in the tree) to
\Delta(\tau^{\prime})
(the value at the node reached at the end of that path).
Note that the idea of recursive updaters was mentioned by
37
, but to our knowledge has not been formalized before.

The constructor
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} mkUpd}}}(\mathsf{cache};\mathsf{update})
builds a tree where the value of the node
y_{n}
reached along the path
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
results from iteratively applying
\mathsf{update}
to the inputs
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, together with the most recent cache, starting from the given
\mathsf{cache}
(see
Fig.

6
).
The application
\mathsf{u}.\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}(\mathsf{dx})
returns the node value in
\mathsf{u}
at the path with single edge
\mathsf{dx}
, and the updater rooted at that node.

4.2.
Program Transformation

Type Transformations.

Our program transformation
\mathcal{I}\!\left\{-\right\}
behaves like the identity on types (see
Fig.

11
), except for closure types, which are translated into incremental versions

\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau\right\}:=\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}\to\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\times\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\kappa\times\sigma);\Delta(\tau))

with updaters that can process both input changes and changes to captured variables in
\kappa
.

Primitives.

For every constant
c:\tau
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
, we assume a corresponding incrementalized version
c_{\mathsf{core}}:\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
.
For instance, consider the function
\mathsf{length}:\mathsf{List}\;\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
.
Its incremental version caches the length and constructs an updater with an update function
\mathsf{updateLength}
:
11
11

11

We omit the empty environment change
\Delta(\kappa)=\Delta(\{\})=\mathsf{Sub}\{\}
for simplicity.

\displaystyle\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} def}}}\ \mathsf{length}_{\mathsf{core}}:=\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}\mathsf{xs}\ldotp\mathsf{len}:=\mathsf{length}(\mathsf{xs});\;(\mathsf{len},\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} mkUpd}}}(\mathsf{len};\mathsf{updateLength}))

\displaystyle\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} def}}}\ \mathsf{updateLength}:=\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}(\mathsf{dxs},\mathsf{cachedLen})\ldotp

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{len}:=\mathsf{cachedLen}+\mathsf{length}(\mathsf{dxs}.\textnormal{insert})-\mathsf{length}(\mathsf{dxs}.\textnormal{remove});

\displaystyle\qquad(\{\textnormal{new:}\,\mathsf{len},\textnormal{same:}\,\mathsf{len}==\mathsf{cachedLen}\},\mathsf{len})

The function
\mathsf{updateLength}
computes the new length
\mathsf{len}
taking insertions and deletions into account.
It returns the length change of type
\Delta(\mathbb{N})
together with the new length to be cached.

Composition.

Suppose we have a function
\mathsf{evens}:\mathsf{List}\;\mathbb{N}\to\mathsf{List}\;\mathbb{N}
that filters out odd numbers from a list and we compose it with the length function:
\mathsf{countEvens}(\mathsf{xs}):=\mathsf{length}(\mathsf{evens}(\mathsf{xs}))
.
Intuitively, to incrementalize it, we can take the incremental versions of the two functions and create a new updater that uses the component updaters as its cache:
12
12

12

This is a simplified version of what our program transformation produces; we again ignore empty environments.

\begin{aligned} &\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} def}}}\ \mathsf{countEvens}_{\mathsf{core}}:=\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}(\mathsf{xs})\ldotp\\
&\qquad(\mathsf{ys},u_{\mathsf{evens}}):=\mathsf{evens}_{\mathsf{core}}(\mathsf{xs});\\
&\qquad(\mathsf{len},u_{\mathsf{length}}):=\mathsf{length}_{\mathsf{core}}(\mathsf{ys});\\
&\qquad(\mathsf{len},\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} mkUpd}}}((u_{\mathsf{evens}},u_{\mathsf{length}});\mathsf{updateCE}))\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} def}}}\ \mathsf{updateCE}:=\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}(\mathsf{dxs},(u_{\mathsf{evens}},u_{\mathsf{length}}))\ldotp\\
&\qquad(\mathsf{dy},u_{\mathsf{evens}}^{\prime}):=u_{\mathsf{evens}}.\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}(\mathsf{dxs});\\
&\qquad(\mathsf{dlen},u_{\mathsf{length}}^{\prime}):=u_{\mathsf{length}}.\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}(\mathsf{dy});\\
&\qquad(\mathsf{dlen},(u_{\mathsf{evens}}^{\prime},u_{\mathsf{length}}^{\prime}))\end{aligned}

Our program transformation automates this logic.
A term
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
is translated to a term
\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\Gamma\right\}\vdash\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma}\!\left\{t\right\}:\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\times\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma));\Delta(\tau))
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
as defined in
Fig.

14
.
The idea is that we must not only be able to evaluate a given term, but also to obtain changes to its evaluation depending on environment changes.
To handle such changes in an updater, we reify them as values of type
\Delta(\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma))
: tuples of changes to the variables in
\Gamma
(using
\mathsf{CtxType}(-)
from
Fig.

4
).

The transformation rule for variables (
Fig.

14
) yields a cache-less updater that simply projects out the variable change from the environment change (using
\pi_{\Gamma\mapsto x}
from
Fig.

12
).
For abstractions
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp t:\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau
, we translate the body
t
in the environment that includes exactly the free variables, which yields an updater
\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\kappa\times\sigma);\Delta(\tau))
for the closure change
\Delta(\kappa)
and the input change
\Delta(\sigma)
.
For applications
s\;t:\tau
, the translation of
s
yields an updater for closure changes and
t
for input changes, which are composed to yield an updater for the application of the closure.
For record access
t.\ell:\tau
, we translate the term
t
to an updater for changes to the record.
However, such a change is a subrecord that may not contain the field
\ell
.
If a trivial change
\mathsf{same}_{\tau}
exists (e.g.,
\{\}
for record types), we can use it to obtain the change to the field.
Otherwise, we have to cache the previous value
r_{l}
and use the trivial change
\mathsf{sameAs}(r_{l})
if
\ell
is not in the record change (see
Fig.

13
).

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}=\sigma\text{ for }\sigma\text{ ground}\qquad\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\times\tau\right\}=\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}\times\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\qquad\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau\right\}=\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}\to\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\times\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\kappa\times\sigma);\Delta(\tau))

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\Gamma\right\}=(x_{1}:\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma_{1}\right\},\dots,x_{n}:\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma_{n}\right\})\text{ for contexts }\Gamma=(x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\sigma_{n})

\displaystyle\Delta(\sigma)=\{\textnormal{new}:\sigma,\textnormal{same}:\mathbb{B}\}\quad\text{ for }\sigma=\mathbb{B},\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R},\mathsf{Name}\qquad\Delta(\sigma\times\tau)=\Delta(\sigma)\times\Delta(\tau)

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathsf{List}\;\sigma)=\{\textnormal{insert}:\mathsf{List}\;(\mathbb{N}\times\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}),\textnormal{remove}:\mathsf{List}\;\mathbb{N},\textnormal{change}:\mathsf{List}\;(\mathbb{N}\times\Delta(\sigma))\}

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathsf{NameMap}\;\sigma)=\{\textnormal{add}:\mathsf{NameMap}\;\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\},\textnormal{remove}:\mathsf{NameSet},\textnormal{change}:\mathsf{NameMap}\;\Delta(\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\Delta(\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\})=\mathsf{Sub}\{\ell_{1}:\Delta(\tau_{1}),\dots,\ell_{n}:\Delta(\tau_{n})\}\qquad\Delta(\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau)=\Delta(\kappa)

function

besides

then

for
