to

\mathsf{length}(\mathsf{inputs})

do

if

\mathsf{i}\notin\mathsf{toVisit}

then

continue

\mathsf{dx}\leftarrow\mathsf{dxs}[\mathsf{i}]
or
\mathsf{sameAs}(\mathsf{inputs}[\mathsf{i}])
if
\mathsf{i}\notin\mathsf{dxs}

(\mathsf{dy},\mathsf{upds}[\mathsf{i}])\leftarrow\mathsf{upds}[\mathsf{i}].\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} apply}}}((\mathsf{d\gamma},\mathsf{dx}))

\mathsf{inputs}[\mathsf{i}]\leftarrow\mathsf{apply}(\mathsf{inputs}[\mathsf{i}],\mathsf{dx})

if

\lnot\mathsf{isSame}(\mathsf{dy})

then

\mathsf{changed}[\mathsf{i}]\leftarrow\mathsf{dy}

\mathsf{cache}\leftarrow\{\textnormal{inputs:}\,\mathsf{inputs},\textnormal{upds:}\,\mathsf{upds}\}

return

(\{\textnormal{changed:}\,\mathsf{changed}\},\mathsf{cache})

Figure 15
.
Pseudocode for incrementalizing simplified for-expressions (without accumulators or
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\;(\cdot)
clauses).
It only handles modifications to list elements (no insertions or deletions) and assumes
\mathit{xs}
is a variable.

Incrementalizing Loops.

A loop
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\;\mathit{xs}\;\{\;t\;\}
can be viewed as a sophisticated, higher-order primitive
\mathsf{loopCombinator}(\mathit{xs},\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp t)
that takes a collection
\mathit{xs}
and a loop body
t
and returns a loop result.
Such a combinator-like primitive can be equipped with its own (complex) incremental version.
To minimize recomputation, the loop combinator caches the values produced at each iteration and maintains mappings between collection indices and the iterations that accessed them.
The collections that are accessed by the loop body are communicated to the system via the
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} using}}}\;(\mathsf{element}:=\mathsf{collection}[\mathsf{index}],\ldots)
clause.
It uses this information, together with incoming change descriptions, to determine which iterations need to be rerun, and thus which entries of the loop’s output must change.
The incrementalization of simple loops (
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\;\mathit{xs}\;\{\;t\;\}
) is presented in
Fig.

15
.
For fully featured loops, this is more complicated and relegated to the appendix.

4.3.
Correctness

Change Validity.

In order to reason about the correctness of the incrementalization, we first need to define when a change is valid.
For this we introduce a type-indexed relation
\mathcal{C}_{\tau}\subseteq\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\Delta(\tau)\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
where
(x,\mathit{dx},x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau}
means that
\mathit{dx}
is a valid change from
x
to
x^{\prime}
.
The relation is defined inductively on types in
Fig.

16
.
For instance,
\mathit{dx}
is a valid change from
x
to
x^{\prime}
for basic types if its “new” field is
x^{\prime}
and its “same” field being
true
implies
x=x^{\prime}
.
Changes to tuples are valid if the individual changes to their components are valid.
Finally, a change to a closure is valid if it is a valid change to its captured environment and the function itself is unchanged.

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\{(x,\mathit{dx},x^{\prime})\in\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\Delta(\sigma)\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\mid\mathit{dx}(\textnormal{new})=x^{\prime}\land(\mathit{dx}(\textnormal{same})=\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} true}}}\implies x=x^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle\quad\text{ for }\sigma=\mathbb{B},\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R},\mathsf{Name}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\sigma\times\tau}

\displaystyle=\{((x,y),(\mathit{dx},\mathit{dy}),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\in\left\llbracket\sigma\times\tau\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\Delta(\sigma\times\tau)\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\sigma\times\tau\right\rrbracket\!\mid(x,\mathit{dx},x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}\land(y,\mathit{dy},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau}

\displaystyle=\{((e,f),\mathit{de},(e^{\prime},f^{\prime}))\in\left\llbracket\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\Delta(\kappa)\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau\right\rrbracket\!\mid(e,\mathit{de},e^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa},f=f^{\prime}\}

Figure 16
.
Relation of valid changes (selected rules).

Updater Validity.

Similarly, we need to define when an updater
\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\sigma);\Delta(\tau))
is valid for a function
f:\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
, previously evaluated at an input
x\in\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
.
To this end, we introduce the validity predicate
\mathcal{U}_{\sigma\leadsto\tau}^{f,x}\subseteq\left\llbracket\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\sigma);\Delta(\tau))\right\rrbracket\!
, defined
coinductively
(as the greatest fixed point):

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\sigma\leadsto\tau}^{f,x}=\{u\in\left\llbracket\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\sigma);\Delta(\tau))\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle\mid\forall\mathit{dx},x^{\prime}\text{ with }(x,\mathit{dx},x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}:(y,\mathit{dy},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau}\land u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{\sigma\leadsto\tau}^{f,x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\text{ where }y=f(x),y^{\prime}=f(x^{\prime}),\text{ and applying $u$ to $\mathit{dx}$ yields }(\mathit{dy},u^{\prime})\}

In words,
u
is a valid updater for the function
f
at the input
x
if for any valid change
\mathit{dx}
from
x
to
x^{\prime}
, applying the updater to
\mathit{dx}
yields a valid output change
\mathit{dy}
from
f(x)
to
f(x^{\prime})
and a new updater
u^{\prime}
that is again valid for
f
, but now at input
x^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\{(x,x^{\prime})\mid x=x^{\prime}\}\text{ (for ground types }\sigma\text{)}\quad\quad\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\times\tau}=\{((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\mid(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}\land(y,y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau}

\displaystyle=\{((e,f),f^{\prime})\mid\forall(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}\ldotp(y,y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}\land u\in\mathcal{U}_{\kappa\times\sigma\leadsto\tau}^{f,(e,x)}\text{ where }y=f(e,x),(y^{\prime},u)=f^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\{(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\mid\forall(x:\sigma)\in\Gamma\ldotp(\gamma(x),\gamma^{\prime}(x))\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}\}

Figure 17
.
Logical relation between
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
and
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
(selected rules).

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{tpl}_{\Gamma}&:\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma)\right\rrbracket\!\\
\mathsf{tpl}_{\bullet}(\emptyset)&=\{\},\quad\mathsf{tpl}_{\Gamma,x:\tau}(\gamma)=(\mathsf{tpl}_{\Gamma}(\gamma\setminus\{x\}),\gamma(x))\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \mathsf{ctx}_{\Gamma}&:\left\llbracket\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma)\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\!\\
\mathsf{ctx}_{\bullet}(\{\})&=\emptyset,\quad\mathsf{ctx}_{\Gamma,x:\tau}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ctx}_{\Gamma}(\pi_{1}(\gamma))[x\mapsto\pi_{2}(\gamma)]\end{aligned}

Figure 18
.
Functions to convert between contexts and tuples.

Logical Relation.

With this machinery in place, we define a logical relation
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}\subseteq\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\right\rrbracket\!
between terms in
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
and
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
, which characterizes when the
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
term is a correct incremental version of the
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
term (
Fig.

17
).
The relation is the identity at ground types and at composite types, it is defined in the standard way: two values are related if all their components are.

The interesting case is closure types
\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau
because they have incremental versions. The semantics of such a closure is
(\mathit{env},f)\in\left\llbracket\kappa\right\rrbracket\!\times(\left\llbracket\kappa\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!)
, consisting of the captured environment and a “function pointer” for computing outputs from inputs and the environment.
Such a closure is translated to an ordinary
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
function
f^{\prime}
of type
\left\llbracket\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau\right\}\right\rrbracket\!=\left\llbracket\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\kappa\times\sigma);\Delta(\tau))\right\rrbracket\!
.
The translation sends transformed inputs to transformed outputs, together with an updater from environment and input changes to output changes.
Now,
(\mathit{env}_{0},f)
and
f^{\prime}
are related if for all related inputs
x_{0}\in\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!,x_{0}^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}\right\rrbracket\!
, letting
y_{0}=f(\mathit{env}_{0},x_{0})
and
(y_{0}^{\prime},u_{0})=f^{\prime}(x_{0}^{\prime})
, we have:
(1) the outputs
y_{0}\in\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!,y_{0}^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\right\rrbracket\!
are related, and
(2) the resulting updater
u_{0}\in\left\llbracket\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\kappa\times\sigma);\Delta(\tau))\right\rrbracket\!
is a valid updater of
f
for the input
(\mathit{env}_{0},x_{0})
.

To state correctness of
\mathcal{I}\!\left\{-\right\}
, we need a few helper functions operating on the tuple representation of contexts (
Fig.

18
).
Using this, we obtain the following result, proven in
Appendix

C
.

Lemma 4.1 (Fundamental Lemma).

Assume
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
, and thus
\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\Gamma\right\}\vdash\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma}\!\left\{t\right\}:\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\times\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma));\Delta(\tau))
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{core}}
.
Then for all
(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
, we have

(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma),\pi_{1}(\left\llbracket\mathcal{I}\!\left\{t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma^{\prime})))\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}\quad\text{ and }\quad\pi_{2}(\left\llbracket\mathcal{I}\!\left\{t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma)\leadsto\tau}^{\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!\circ\mathsf{ctx}_{\Gamma},\mathsf{tpl}_{\Gamma}(\gamma)}

Considering the special case of one-element contexts and unfolding the definition of the validity predicate for updaters to handle sequences of input changes, one obtains the following corollary:

Corollary 4.2.

Let
x:\sigma\vdash t:\tau
be a
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
term denoting a function
f:\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
, for ground types
\sigma
,
\tau
.
Let
g=\left\llbracket\mathcal{I}\!\left\{t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!
and
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots\in\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
be a sequence of inputs.
Let
\mathit{dx}_{0},\mathit{dx}_{1},\mathit{dx}_{2},\ldots\in\left\llbracket\Delta(\sigma)\right\rrbracket\!
be valid change descriptions:
(x_{i},\mathit{dx}_{i},x_{i+1})\in\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}
.
Finally, let
(y_{0},u_{0})=g(x_{0})
, and for all
i
, let
(\mathit{dy}_{i},u_{i+1})
be the result of applying
u_{i}
to
\mathit{dx}_{i}
.
Then we have
y_{0}=f(x_{0})
and
(f(x_{i}),\mathit{dy}_{i},f(x_{i+1}))\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau}
for all
i
.

5.
Case Studies

We have implemented a prototype of our approach by embedding the
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
and
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
DSLs in Julia. We investigate the impact of incrementalization across a variety of models and custom MCMC kernels. We also compare to Gen
(
13
;
12
)
, a state-of-the-art PPL with incrementalization.

Models and Kernels.

To test our approach, we implement 16 MCMC kernels in 6 Bayesian models. (1)
Robust regression
(
8
)
:
Bayesian linear regression with outliers. We implement Gaussian drift moves on the parameters and Metropolis-Hastings moves on outlier indicators.
(2)
Binary Gaussian mixture
(
16
)
:
2D Gaussian mixture model with inferred parameters, mixing weight, and cluster assignments. Kernels update parameters and mixing weights, or one cluster assignment.
(3)
Hidden Markov Model with Inferred Parameters
(
42
)
:
A hidden Markov model with an a priori unknown transition matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{100\times 100}
, and continuous observations. Our MCMC kernels update a single row of the transition matrix or a single latent state.
(4)
Mixture of Finite Mixtures
(
34
)
:
A 2D Gaussian mixture model where the number of clusters is unknown with Poisson prior. Empty birth/death moves create new empty clusters or remove unoccupied clusters. Singleton birth/death moves propose to split a datapoint into its own singleton cluster, or to merge a singleton cluster into an existing cluster. Cluster parameter changes propose updates to a single latent cluster’s parameters. Mixing weight changes re-propose the mixing proportions of the current latent clusters. Cluster assignment changes reassign a single datapoint to a different (pre-existing) cluster.
(5)
Latent Dirichlet Allocation
(
6
)
:
A topic model. We consider MCMC moves that change the theme (topic mixture) of a single document, the lexicon (word mixture) of a single topic, or the topic assignment for a single word.
(6)
Dirichlet Process Mixture Model
(
38
)
:
A nonparametric mixture model with infinitely many clusters, marginalized via a Chinese Restaurant Process. We consider Gibbs updates to cluster assignments and parameters as described in Algorithm 8 of
38
.

Systems.

We implement each model both in our Julia embedding of
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
, and in Gen’s static modeling language, with Gen’s experimental support for fine-grained incremental computation turned on (using the
@gen (static, diffs) function
syntax). We implement each inference kernel in Julia, using three different strategies for computing the necessary model densities: (1) our own implementation of incremental density computation, (2) our own implementation of full density recomputation, and (3) Gen’s implementation of incremental density computation.

Figure 20
.
Linear scaling plots for entire MCMC parameter sweeps.

Results.

Table
1
presents absolute and relative runtimes of each MCMC kernel, for each approach to density (re)computation. Timings were recorded for each application of each kernel during 100 steps of MCMC inference on datasets of fixed size (chosen separately per model), and the median across all applications of a given kernel is reported. The log-log scaling plots in
Fig.

19
show how median times for executing a single kernel scale with dataset size. Each iteration of MCMC inference may execute
O(N)
kernels (e.g., in a mixture model, to consider reassigning each datapoint’s cluster assignment, then updating cluster parameters);
Figure

20
shows how these aggregate MCMC moves (and thus end-to-end inference) scale with the number of datapoints.

Findings.

The log-log scaling plots in Fig.
19
are representative of three patterns across our experiments. (a) In many cases, our approach successfully reduces the asymptotic complexity of an update (e.g., from linear-time to constant-time), but so does Gen’s state-of-the-art incrementalization, often with better constant factors. (b) In rare cases (see red entries in
Table

1
), the density must be fully recomputed by all three systems, and our approach to incrementalization adds overhead relative to the non-incremental version. (c) In some cases (see green entries in
Table

1
), our approach delivers asymptotic savings not captured by Gen’s state-of-the-art incrementalization. Our approach is the only one that achieves the desired asymptotic complexity for all updates.
Figure

20
illustrates how savings on individual updates affect the runtime of an MCMC algorithm that composes these updates into realistic schedules (e.g., “run one parameter update and
N
cluster assignment updates per iteration”). The plots again surface several interesting patterns. (a) In some cases, our approach reduces the cost of an update from linear to constant-time, enabling it to be efficiently run on every datapoint at every iteration. The failure of Gen to incrementalize these updates leads to
O(N^{2})
runtime overall. (b) When a single row of an HMM’s transition matrix is changed, our approach (but not Gen’s) efficiently revisits only the time steps that executed the changed transition. This leads to an
O(K)
reduction in runtime per step. But this is just a constant-factor speed-up with respect to the sequence length. (c) In a Dirichlet process mixture, the expected number of latent clusters grows logarithmically with
N
, so each Gibbs cluster assignment move requires more density queries as
N
increases. This means even our approach scales super-linearly, despite reducing the cost of each density evaluation to a constant. This is even more noticeable without incrementalization.

Soundness.

We found that Gen sometimes crashed, silently computed incorrect densities, or resorted to full recomputation, requiring various workarounds.
For example, our initial GMM program sampled cluster assignments and observations in separate loops.
13
13

13

By “loop” we mean an application of Gen’s
Map
or
Unfold
combinator to a kernel implementing the loop body.
We had to merge the loops to achieve
O(1)
assignment updates in Gen.
Conversely, we had to sample the latent means and covariances in
separate
loops, because Gen did not correctly track changes to tuples.

Implementation Details.

Our implementation faithfully realizes the algorithmic choices formalized in
Sections

2
,
3
and
4
, but differs in implementation details due to our choice of Julia as a host language (chosen for its scientific computing ecosystem and for fair comparison with Gen).
While our formalism uses explicit program transformations, in Julia we use a combination of macros, nonstandard interpretation, and function overloading via multiple dispatch to achieve the same effect.
This is a common way of embedding DSLs in Julia, also used in Gen
(
13
)
and Turing
(
17
)
.
Our implementation features an abstract
Updater
class with separate subclasses for each primitive operation, where each subclass stores an operation-specific cache and has an
update
method corresponding to applying the updater to an input change.
In alignment with our theoretical presentation, the implementation uses immutable, persistent collections (including for its caches).

6.
Related Work and Discussion

Incrementalizing
\lambda
-Calculi.

Our work is inspired by the incremental
\lambda
-calculus of
9
, which introduces the notion of change types and an incrementalizing program transformation.
Their change type for functions
\sigma\to\tau
is
\sigma\to\Delta(\sigma)\to\Delta(\tau)
, which is more general than our closure-based approach.
However, their work does not support caching.

18
add caching of intermediate results in what they call
cache-transfer style
.
They work in the untyped
\lambda
-calculus in order to avoid tracking cache types.
They require programs to be preprocessed into A’-normal form and lambda-lifted.
32
solve the typing issues in the first-order setting using existential types for the caches.
37
instead incrementalizes functions
\sigma\to\tau
to
caching derivative-associated
functions
\sigma\to\tau\times(\Delta\sigma\to\Delta\tau)
, where intermediate results are stored in the inner closure’s environment, so the caches do not need to be represented in the types.
He also mentions that a series of modifications can be supported via “recursively-caching” functions, but does not elaborate.
This was inspiration for our updater types.

Dynamic Dependency Tracking.

An influential
dynamic
incrementalization technique is self-adjusting computation
(
1
)
, where the initial run produces a computation trace memoizing intermediate results.
Changed inputs require only the recomputation of affected parts of the trace (see
(
2
)
).
Adapton
(
20
)
instead constructs a demand-driven dependency graph at runtime.
Follow-up work
(
19
)
also uses first-class names, recognizing them as a “critical linguistic feature for efficient incremental computation”.
Whereas they use unique names to identify subcomputations, we use them to identify domain entities in probabilistic models.

Incrementalization in Probabilistic Programming.

Several program transformations aim to speed up single-site Metropolis-Hastings specifically.
Shred
(
49
)
optimizes changes to random choices that preserve the control flow by compiling to a straight-line program and only recomputing its transitive dependencies.
C3
(
39
)
avoids recomputation by combining continuation-passing style with call-site caching.
Similarly,
10
describe a translation from a probabilistic program to an event structure, tracking dependencies and avoiding unnecessary sampling.

For more complex inference algorithms, many systems analyze probabilistic programs using graphical models because changing a variable only affects its
Markov blanket
.
An early instance of this is BLOG
(
33
)
, a declarative PPL for open-universe models, whose Metropolis-Hastings inference maintains a dynamic dependency graph to avoid recomputing factors in the acceptance ratio.
As changing a variable can change the dependency graph, proposals require recomputing the dependency structure.
The Bean Machine
(
45
)
extends this with programmable proposal distributions that can be composed and blocked across variables.
Venture
(
29
;
28
)
was perhaps the first to introduce dynamic dependency tracking for a higher-order generative language with programmable inference, enabling optimal asymptotic scaling for a broad range of inference algorithms.
Its dependency tracking also handles aliasing from memoization and was the first to be extended to sublinear-time inference updates, by subsampling dependencies.

7
present a program analysis to factorize the density function in the presence of loops, which can speed up single-site MH, black-box variational inference, and sequential Monte Carlo.

Gen
(
13
)
allows users to customize incrementalization via an
update
function in its generative function interface.
By default, its static modeling language (for straight-line probabilistic programs) automates incremental updates via dependency tracking and partial evaluation.
It also has undocumented, experimental support for incrementalization based on “diffs”
(
14
)
, disabled by default due to bugs and limited support.
Our work drew inspiration from Gen, and could inform future extensions to Gen, e.g., to handle features such as names, tuples, and higher-order functions.

25
also aim to improve the efficiency of density computations in probabilistic programs, but via auto-vectorization based on tensor operations rather than incremental computation; notably, incrementalization gives asymptotic speedups even on a single CPU, without parallel hardware.
Instead of incremental density evaluation,
11
tackle the related problem of incrementally generating
samples
for changes to the input
program
by translating their traces.

Density Semantics.

22
define both measure and density semantics for an imperative, first-order probabilistic language, but their reference measure only supports densities when all primitive distributions are continuous, whereas our traces may contain heterogeneous types.

44
present a verified compilation pipeline from a simplified version of Stan to optimized density code in C, but their language and approach (operational semantics, imperative) are very different from ours.

5
use a similar semantics to ours, but their language does not feature name generation or unordered collections—key contributions of ours.

Nonparametric Models.

Existing systems struggle with exchangeable sequences and nonparametric models.
Gen
(
13
)
can express nonparametric models, but its traces introduce artificial orderings on exchangeable sequences, making incrementalization less effective because insertions and deletions shift indices.

31
automate involutive MCMC (which can express many MCMC algorithms) for open-universe models, but have to deal with complicated and error-prone bookkeeping of indices into variable-length sequences.
By contrast, our approach of unordered collections with name-based access offers stable identifiers for elements of exchangeable sequences, simplifying the implementation and improving the effectiveness of incrementalization.

Limitations.

Long, straight-line programs made of many individually inexpensive operations—e.g., many large Bayes nets—do not benefit from incrementalization in our approach, which must still execute an incrementalized version of each line of such a program.
As it stands, our approach is also limited to loops over finite data structures.
Extending our implementation to handle general
while
loops would be straightforward, requiring only small changes to our existing density and incrementalization transformations.
General recursion would be more challenging, as traces of recursive probabilistic programs would need to be assigned
recursive trace types
, for which we would need to define new change representations and incrementalization rules.
Whether via recursion or while loops, the possibility of non-termination would complicate our semantics and correctness proofs (requiring, e.g., the use of quasi-Borel
predomains

(
46
)
).

Extensions.

We have focused on MCMC, but incremental computation could also benefit other Monte Carlo inference algorithms (see, e.g., the SMC pseudocode in
Fig.

2(e)
).
For variational inference, the fit is less natural: traces are independently sampled rather than incrementally modified. Our pipeline works by generating
deterministic
density programs and incrementalizing them. However, it is common to
stochastically estimate
densities that would be intractable to compute exactly
(
3
)
. It would be interesting to develop versions of incrementalization that work for stochastic computations, perhaps building on ideas from
11
.

Data Availability Statement

The artifact for this paper (consisting of the Julia implementation and benchmarks) is archived on Zenodo
(
50
)
.

We are also grateful to Cameron Freer and the anonymous reviewers for their helpful feedback, which improved the presentation of the paper.

Typing Rules. We write
\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma)
for the product of the types appearing in
\Gamma
;
\Gamma|_{S}
for the restriction of
\Gamma
to the variables in
S
; and
\mathsf{FV}(t)
for the set of free variables in
t
.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathbb{B}\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=\{0,1\}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathbb{R}\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=[0,1]

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\sigma\times\tau\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{List}\;\sigma\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!^{n}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{NameMap}\;\tau\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=\{f:A\to\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!\mid A\subseteq\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!\text{ finite}\}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}\right\rrbracket\!

.

A.1.
Denotational Semantics for
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
in
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}

The main paper gives a semantics of
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
in the category of quasi-Borel spaces. Some additional structure is useful for reasoning about names. We develop this extended semantic setting here.

Intuition.

The key idea is to define, for each type
\tau
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
, a family of subspaces
\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U}\subseteq\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
, indexed by countable sets
U\subseteq[0,1]
.
14
14

14

We also allow
U=[0,1]
, with
\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U}=\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
. When
U\subseteq V
, we ensure
\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U}\subseteq\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{V}
, and for all
U,V
,
\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U\cap V}=\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U}\cap\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{V}
. Such indexed families form a cartesian-closed category
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
, with morphisms
f:X\to Y
satisfying
f(X_{U})\subseteq Y_{U}
; we develop this formally below.
Intuitively,
\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U}
contains only those values of
\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!
that use
at most
the names in
U
. Thus, types that do not use names (e.g.,
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{R}
) have
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!_{U}=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
for all
U
, whereas
\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!_{U}=U
. Products and other containers use names at most in
U
if all their elements do. Given two indexed families
X_{U}\subseteq X
and
Y_{U}\subseteq Y
, we can define the indexed family
(X\Rightarrow Y)_{U}\subseteq X\Rightarrow Y
by
\{f\in X\Rightarrow Y\mid\forall\text{ countable }V\subseteq[0,1],\,\forall x\in X_{V},f(x)\in Y_{U\cup V}\}
. We use this construction to interpret function types: it encodes that a function uses a name if it can return values that use that name even on inputs that do not use the name. Given an indexed family
X_{U}\subseteq X
, we can also construct an indexed family
(\mathsf{Prob}~X)_{U}\subseteq\mathsf{Prob}~X
, as follows. First, following
15
, define
\Omega\coloneq[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
to be the quasi-Borel space of rose trees, which admits a uniform probability measure
\mu_{\Omega}
. Then define the indexed family of subsets
\Omega\supseteq\Omega_{U}\coloneq U^{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
. Then
(\mathsf{Prob}~X)_{U}\subseteq\mathsf{Prob}~X
is the set of all probability measures
\mu=g_{*}\mu_{\Omega}
that arise as pushforwards by some
g\in(\Omega\Rightarrow X)_{U}
. Intuitively, a probability measure uses a name if the event that the outcome uses that name occurs with positive probability. Probability measures do not use the
fresh
names they generate, which are continuous samples and each occur with probability 0. Together with the function space definition, the
\mathsf{Prob}
construction lets us define
\left\llbracket\mathsf{P}\,\sigma\,\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U}=\{(\mu,f)\mid f\in\left\llbracket\sigma\to\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U},\mu\in(\mathsf{Prob}~\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!)_{U}\}
.

We develop this perspective more formally below, showing how these indexed families of quasi-Borel spaces arise as the objects of a particular functor category.

Definition A.1 (
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
).

\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
is the full subcategory of
[\mathcal{A},\mathsf{Qbs}]

containing functors that preserve pullbacks,
where
\mathsf{Qbs}
is the category of quasi-Borel spaces
(
21
)

and
\mathcal{A}
is the category whose objects are countable subsets of the interval
[0,1]
, as well as the interval
[0,1]
itself, and whose morphisms are given by subset inclusion.

Proposition A.2 (
\mathcal{A}
is bicartesian).

The category
\mathcal{A}
is finitely complete and finitely cocomplete.

Proof.

Clearly,
[0,1]
is terminal in
\mathcal{A}
, and pullbacks in
\mathcal{A}
are given by set intersection.
Hence,
\mathcal{A}
is finitely complete
(
27
, Exercise III.4.10)
.
Dually,
\emptyset
is initial in
\mathcal{A}
,
and pushouts in
\mathcal{A}
are given by set union.
This means that
\mathcal{A}
is finitely cocomplete.
∎

Proposition A.3 (
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
is complete).

The category
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
is (small) complete,
with limits given pointwise.
Moreover, the inclusion functor
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{A},\mathsf{Qbs}]

preserves these limits.

Proof.

Consider a pullback diagram
F:(\leftarrow\cdot\to)\to\mathcal{A}

and a diagram
G:\mathcal{J}\to[\mathcal{A},\mathsf{Qbs}]
,
where
\mathcal{J}
is a small category
and
G(j)
is a pullback-preserving functor in
[\mathcal{A},\mathsf{Qbs}]
for all
j
in
\mathcal{J}
.
Now, define
H=\lim_{j\in\mathcal{J}}G(j)
.
We may then compute:

\displaystyle H\left(\lim_{X\in\leftarrow\cdot\to}F(X)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\lim_{j\in\mathcal{J}}G(j)\right)\left(\lim_{X\in\leftarrow\cdot\to}F(X)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{j\in\mathcal{J}}G(j)\left(\lim_{X\in\leftarrow\cdot\to}F(X)\right)

limits computed pointwise
(
26
, §I)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\lim_{X\in\leftarrow\cdot\to}G(j)(F(X))

\displaystyle G(j)\textrm{ preserves pullbacks}

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{X\in\leftarrow\cdot\to}\lim_{j\in\mathcal{J}}G(j)(F(X))

interchange of limits
(
27
, §IX)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{X\in\leftarrow\cdot\to}\left(\lim_{j\in\mathcal{J}}G(j)\right)(F(X))

limits computed pointwise

\displaystyle=\lim_{X\in\leftarrow\cdot\to}H(F(X))

Note,
limits in
[\mathcal{A},\mathsf{Qbs}]

are computed pointwise
because
\mathsf{Qbs}
is (small) complete
(
21
;
30
;
4
)
.
Thus,
H:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{Qbs}
preserves pullbacks.
This means that
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}

is complete
and inherits all limits
from
[\mathcal{A},\mathsf{Qbs}]
.
∎

Proposition A.4.

There is a faithful functor
\mathcal{C}:\nu\mathsf{Qbs}\to\mathsf{Qbs}

that maps each functor
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{Qbs}
to
F[0,1]

and each natural transformation
\alpha:F\to G
to
\alpha_{[0,1]}:F[0,1]\to G[0,1]
.

Proof.

\mathcal{C}
clearly forms a functor.
To show that
\mathcal{C}
is faithful,
suppose that
\alpha_{[0,1]}=\beta_{[0,1]}

for two natural transformations
\alpha,\beta:F\to G
.
We aim to show that this implies
\alpha=\beta
—or, equivalently,
that
\alpha_{U}=\beta_{U}
for all
U
in
\mathcal{A}
.
Because
\alpha
and
\beta
are natural, the following two diagrams commute:
F(U)
F[0,1]
G(U)
G[0,1]
F(\subseteq)
G(\subseteq)
\alpha_{U}
\alpha_{[0,1]}
F(U)
F[0,1]
G(U)
G[0,1]
F(\subseteq)
G(\subseteq)
\beta_{U}
\beta_{[0,1]}

for any
U
in
\mathcal{A}
.
The above diagrams then imply that:

G(\subseteq)\circ\alpha_{U}=\alpha_{[0,1]}\circ F(\subseteq)=\beta_{[0,1]}\circ F(\subseteq)=G(\subseteq)\circ\beta_{U}

Since
F
and
G
preserve pullbacks, they also preserve monomorphisms
(
26
, §II)
.
In particular,

G(\subseteq)
is monic.
Hence, we may conclude that
\alpha_{U}=\beta_{U}
.
∎

Corollary A.5 (
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
is concrete).

There is a faithful functor
\_:\nu\mathsf{Qbs}\to\mathsf{Set}

that maps each functor
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{Qbs}
to
|F[0,1]|

and each natural transformation
\alpha:F\to G

to
|\alpha_{[0,1]}|:|F[0,1]|\to|G[0,1]|
.
In other words, the category
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
is concrete.

Proof.

By the previous proposition, the functor
that maps each
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{Qbs}
to
F[0,1]
is faithful.
It is also well-known that mapping each quasi-Borel space
(X,M_{X})

to
X
—i.e., mapping the corresponding concrete sheaf
X:\mathsf{Sbs}\to\mathsf{Set}
to
X(1)
—also forms a faithful functor
(
4
, Proposition 28)
.
Hence, their composition is faithful.
∎

Proposition A.6 (Coproducts computed pointwise).

Consider objects
F,G:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{Qbs}
in
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
.
Their coproduct
F+G:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{Qbs}

can be computed pointwise:

(F+G)(U)=F(U)+G(U)

Proposition A.7 (
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
is cartesian closed).

The category
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
is cartesian closed,
with the exponential object
F^{G}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{Qbs}

given by:

F^{G}(U)=\{h\in\mathsf{Qbs}(G[0,1],F[0,1])\mid\forall V\in\mathcal{A},h(G(V))\subseteq F(U\cup V)\}

viewed as a subobject of the
\mathsf{Qbs}
exponential
F[0,1]^{G[0,1]}
.

Definition A.8 (Rose trees).

Let
\Omega=[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
denote the
quasi-Borel space of rose trees
(
15
, §5.4.1)

and take
\mu_{\Omega}
to be the uniform probability
distribution on
\Omega
.
The space
\Omega
extends to a functor:

\Omega(U)=\Bigl\{\omega\in\Omega\mid\forall\rho\in\mathbb{N}^{*},\omega(\rho)\in U\Bigr\}

Definition A.9 (Probability monad).

The
probability monad
on
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
is given by the quadruple
(\mathcal{P},\eta,\mu,\tau)
, where the endofunctor
\mathcal{P}:\nu\mathsf{Qbs}\to\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
is defined pointwise by

\mathcal{P}(F)(U)=\Bigl\{f_{*}\mu_{\Omega}\mid f\in(\Omega\Rightarrow F)(U)\Bigr\}

and the natural transformations
\eta
,
\mu
, and
\tau
for each
U
are the appropriate restrictions of the unit, multiplication, and strength of the Qbs probability monad.

Definition A.10 (Trace probability functor).

For each
F
in
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
, the
trace probability functor

is given by
\mathcal{T}(F,-):\nu\mathsf{Qbs}\to\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
,
defined pointwise by:

\mathcal{T}(F,G)(U)=\mathcal{P}(F)(U)\times(F\Rightarrow G)(U)

In
Figs.

27
and
28
, we give a denotational semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket\!
to the types and terms of
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
. Types are interpreted as objects in
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
, and terms as morphisms (i.e., natural transformations). For readability,
we present the
\mathsf{Set}

concretization
of each term interpretation
(given by the faithful functor
\_:\nu\mathsf{Qbs}\to\mathsf{Set}
). Note that for each
U
, we have
\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash t:\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U}=\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash t:\tau\right\rrbracket\!|_{\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\!(U)}
. Throughout, when
\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)
is a pair (as for
t:\mathsf{P}\,\sigma\,\tau
, where it is a distribution-and-return-map pair), we write
\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!_{i}:=\pi_{i}\circ\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!
for the
i
-th projection.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathbb{B}\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{B},\mathsf{Meas}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{B}))

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}\textrm{ standard Borel}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathbb{R}\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}\textrm{ standard Borel}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{Name}\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle=(U,\mathsf{Meas}(\mathbb{R},U))

\displaystyle U\textrm{ standard Borel (discrete or $[0,1]$)}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\sigma\times\tau\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!(U)\times\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{List}\;\sigma\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!^{n}(U)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\tau\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!,\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!)(U)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\tau\to_{\sigma}\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle=(\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!\Rightarrow\left\llbracket\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!)(U)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}\right\rrbracket\!(U)

\displaystyle=\prod_{(\ell_{i}:\tau_{i})\in\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}}\left\llbracket\tau_{i}\right\rrbracket\!(U)

Figure 27
.
Semantics of
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
types as
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
objects.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash x:\tau\right\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=\pi_{(x,\tau)}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash(s,t):\sigma\times\tau\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=(\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma),\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma))

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash t.i:\tau_{i}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma))

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} if}}}\;s\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} then}}}\;t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} else}}}\;t^{\prime}:\sigma\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)&\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)=\top\\
\left\llbracket t^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp{t}:\tau\to_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=v\mapsto\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma,v)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash s\;t:\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma))

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;t:\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\{\}\,\tau\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=(\delta_{\left\llbracket\{\}\right\rrbracket\!},\_\mapsto\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma))

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t:\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\sigma\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{1}(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)),\mathsf{id}_{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!})

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash x\leftarrow t;s:\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\{\cdots\}\,\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \bigl(&\rho\leftsquigarrow(\pi_{1}\circ\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!)(\gamma);v=(\pi_{2}\circ\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!)(\gamma)(\rho);\\
&\rho^{\prime}\leftsquigarrow(\pi_{1}\circ\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!)(\gamma,v);\mathsf{ret}~(\rho\mathbin{+\!\!+}\rho^{\prime}),\\
&\rho\mapsto(\pi_{2}\circ\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!)(\gamma,(\pi_{2}\circ\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!)(\gamma)(\rho|_{L_{1}}))(\rho|_{L_{2}})\bigr)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} @}}}\;\ell:\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\{\ell:\sigma\}\,\tau\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \bigl(&(x\mapsto\{\ell\mapsto x\})_{*}((\pi_{1}\circ\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!)(\gamma)),\\
&u\mapsto(\pi_{2}\circ\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!)(\gamma)(\pi_{\ell}u)\bigr)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Range}}}\;t\;\{\;s\;\}:\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,(\mathsf{List}\;\sigma)\,(\mathsf{List}\;\tau)\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} (&N\leftsquigarrow\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!_{1}(\gamma);\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!_{1}(\gamma,i),\\
&\rho\mapsto(\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!_{2}(\gamma,i)(\pi_{i}\rho))_{i=1}^{|\rho|})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fresh}~t:\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\mathsf{NameSet}\,\{\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \bigl(&n\leftsquigarrow(\pi_{1}\circ\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!)(\gamma);u\leftsquigarrow\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{[0,1]};\mathsf{ret}~(\mathsf{sort}(u)),\\
&\_\mapsto\{\}\bigr)\end{aligned}

Figure 28
.
Semantics of
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
terms as
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
morphisms. For readability, we present the
\mathsf{Set}
concretization of each interpretation,
\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash t:\tau\right\rrbracket\!=\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash t:\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{[0,1]}
. In general,
\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash t:\tau\right\rrbracket\!_{U}=\left\llbracket\Gamma\vdash t:\tau\right\rrbracket\!|_{\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\!(U)}
.

B.1.
Stock Measures

As presented in
Section

3
, we define for each ground type
\sigma
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
a family of reference measures
\nu_{U}^{\sigma}
, indexed by
countable
name sets
U
.

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{counting}_{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!}

\displaystyle\sigma\in\{\mathbb{B},\mathbb{N}\}

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{counting}_{U}+\Lambda_{[0,1]}

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\sigma\times\tau}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &\rho_{1}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\sigma};\\
&\rho_{2}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U\cup\mathsf{names}(\rho_{1})}^{\tau};\\
&\mathsf{ret}~(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}}

\displaystyle=\nu_{U}^{\tau_{1}\times\cdots\times\tau_{n}}

\displaystyle(\nu_{U}^{\{\}}=\delta_{\left\llbracket\{\}\right\rrbracket\!})

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{List}\;\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\iota_{n})_{*}\nu_{U}^{\sigma^{n}}

\displaystyle(\textsf{with coproduct injections }\iota_{n})

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{NameMap}\;\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\kappa_{n})_{*}(\nu_{U}^{(\mathsf{Name}\times\sigma)^{n}})

\displaystyle(\textsf{with coproduct injections }\kappa_{n})

Symmetry of product reference measures.

A footnote in
Section

3
mentions that the product clause above is order-independent. We give a proof below.

Lemma B.1.

For all countable
U\subseteq[0,1]
and all ground types
\sigma,\tau
,

\nu_{U}^{\sigma\times\tau}=\mathsf{swap}_{*}\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\sigma},

where
\mathsf{swap}(x,y)=(y,x)
.

Proof.

We define the shorthand
P(\sigma,\tau)
for the proposition that

\forall U\subseteq[0,1]\text{ countable},\;\forall h:\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\text{ measurable},

\int h(a,b)\,\nu_{U}^{\sigma\times\tau}(\mathrm{d}(a,b))=\int h(a,b)\,(\mathsf{swap}_{*}\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\sigma})(\mathrm{d}(a,b)).

The lemma states that this is true for all ground types
\sigma
and
\tau
.
We prove this by structural induction on the pair of types
(\sigma,\tau)
. Note that
P(\sigma,\tau)\iff P(\tau,\sigma)
:

\mathsf{swap}\circ\mathsf{swap}=\mathsf{id}
, so

\nu_{U}^{\sigma\times\tau}=\mathsf{swap}_{*}\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\sigma}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\sigma}=\mathsf{swap}_{*}\nu_{U}^{\sigma\times\tau}.

by applying
\mathsf{swap}_{*}
to each side of the equality.
For convenience, we write
N(x):=\mathsf{names}(x)
.

(1)

Base cases.

•

Base case 1.
If
\sigma\in\{\{\},\mathbb{B},\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}\}
, then
N(x)=\emptyset
for all
x\in\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
, and
\nu^{\sigma}_{U}=\nu^{\sigma}_{V}
for all
U,V
. By the definition of
\nu_{U}^{\sigma\times\tau}
, we have:

\nu_{U}^{\sigma\times\tau}(\mathrm{d}(a,b))=\nu_{U}^{\sigma}(\mathrm{d}a)\,\nu_{U\cup N(a)}^{\tau}(\mathrm{d}b)=\nu^{\sigma}_{U}(\mathrm{d}a)\,\nu^{\tau}_{U}(\mathrm{d}b),

where the second equality follows because
N(a)=\emptyset
. Similarly,

\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\sigma}(\mathrm{d}(b,a))=\nu_{U}^{\tau}(\mathrm{d}b)\,\nu_{U\cup N(b)}^{\sigma}(\mathrm{d}a)=\nu^{\tau}_{U}(\mathrm{d}b)\,\nu^{\sigma}_{U}(\mathrm{d}a),

where the second equality follows because
\nu^{\sigma}_{U}=\nu^{\sigma}_{U\cup N(b)}
.
By commutativity of the Qbs measure monad,
\nu^{\sigma}_{U}\otimes\nu^{\tau}_{U}=\mathsf{swap}_{*}(\nu^{\tau}_{U}\otimes\nu^{\sigma}_{U})
.
The case where
\tau\in\{\{\},\mathbb{B},\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}\}
follows by symmetry.

•

Base case 2.
Suppose
\sigma=\tau=\mathsf{Name}
.
Using
\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}=\mathsf{counting}(U)+\Lambda_{[0,1]}
and
N(a)=\{a\}
:

\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}\times\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}(a,b))=\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}a)\,\nu_{U\cup\{a\}}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}b)=\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}a)\bigl(\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}b)+\delta_{a}(\mathrm{d}b)\bigr).

Therefore, for measurable
h\geq 0
,

\int h\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}\times\mathsf{Name}}=\iint h(a,b)\,\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}b)\,\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}a)+\int h(a,a)\,\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}a).

The same expansion is obtained for

\int h\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{swap}_{*}\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}\times\mathsf{Name}})
:

\displaystyle\int h\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{swap}_{*}\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}\times\mathsf{Name}})

\displaystyle=\iint h(a,b)\,\nu_{U\cup\{b\}}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}a)\,\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle=\iint h(a,b)\,\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}a)\,\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}b)+\int h(b,b)\,\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{Name}}(\mathrm{d}b).

(2)

Product constructor.

Assume
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}
and
\tau
is a ground type.
Assume by induction that
P(\sigma_{1},\tau)
and
P(\sigma_{2},\tau)
hold.
Let
h:\left\llbracket\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be measurable.
Expanding
\nu_{U}^{(\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2})\times\tau}
:

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle:=\int h((x,y),z)\,\nu_{U}^{(\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2})\times\tau}(\mathrm{d}((x,y),z))

\displaystyle=\iiint h((x,y),z)\,\nu_{U\cup N(x)\cup N(y)}^{\tau}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\nu_{U\cup N(x)}^{\sigma_{2}}(\mathrm{d}y)\,\nu_{U}^{\sigma_{1}}(\mathrm{d}x).

Here we used
N((x,y))=N(x)\cup N(y)
and unfolded both product clauses.
Apply
P(\sigma_{2},\tau)
at base set
U\cup N(x)
to the inner two integrals,
with the measurable function
h_{x}(y,z):=h((x,y),z)
(for a fixed
x
). This allows us to switch the order in which
z
and
y
are integrated:

I=\iiint h((x,y),z)\,\nu_{U\cup N(x)\cup N(z)}^{\sigma_{2}}(\mathrm{d}y)\,\nu_{U\cup N(x)}^{\tau}(\mathrm{d}z)\,\nu_{U}^{\sigma_{1}}(\mathrm{d}x).

Now apply
P(\sigma_{1},\tau)
at base set
U
to the measurable function

g(x,z):=\int h((x,y),z)\,\nu_{U\cup N(x)\cup N(z)}^{\sigma_{2}}(\mathrm{d}y),

to obtain

I=\iiint h((x,y),z)\,\nu_{U\cup N(z)\cup N(x)}^{\sigma_{2}}(\mathrm{d}y)\,\nu_{U\cup N(z)}^{\sigma_{1}}(\mathrm{d}x)\,\nu_{U}^{\tau}(\mathrm{d}z).

The inner two integrals are exactly
\nu_{U\cup N(z)}^{\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}}
by definition, so

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int h((x,y),z)\,\nu_{U}^{\tau\times(\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2})}(\mathrm{d}(z,(x,y)))

\displaystyle=\int h\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{swap}_{*}\nu_{U}^{\tau\times(\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2})}).

Thus
P(\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2},\tau)
holds.
The case where the right component is a product follows by symmetry.

(3)

List constructor.

Assume
\sigma=\mathsf{List}\;\rho
, and
\tau
is some ground type (the case with the list on the right is symmetric).
Define
\rho^{0}:=\{\}
and
\rho^{n+1}:=\rho^{n}\times\rho
.
Let
\iota_{n}:\left\llbracket\rho^{n}\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\mathsf{List}\;\rho\right\rrbracket\!
be the
n
-th coproduct injection.
The list reference measure is

\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{List}\;\rho}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\iota_{n})_{*}\nu_{U}^{\rho^{n}}.

Also, by definition of
N
on lists, for every
x\in\left\llbracket\rho^{n}\right\rrbracket\!
,

N(\iota_{n}(x))=N(x).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{List}\;\rho\times\tau}

\displaystyle=\left(a\leftsquigarrow\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\iota_{n})_{*}\nu_{U}^{\rho^{n}};\;z\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U\cup N(a)}^{\tau};\;\mathsf{ret}~(a,z)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\iota_{n}\times\mathsf{id}_{\tau})_{*}\,\nu_{U}^{\rho^{n}\times\tau}.

Likewise,

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\mathsf{List}\;\rho}

\displaystyle=\left(z\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\tau};\;a\leftsquigarrow\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\iota_{n})_{*}\nu_{U\cup N(z)}^{\rho^{n}};\;\mathsf{ret}~(z,a)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\mathsf{id}_{\tau}\times\iota_{n})_{*}\,\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\rho^{n}}.

We first prove, by induction on
n
, the auxiliary claim

C_{n}:\quad P(\rho^{n},\tau).

For
n=0
,
\rho^{0}=\{\}
, and
C_{0}=P(\{\},\tau)
has already been covered as a base case in the overall proof.
For the inductive case, suppose
C_{n}
by induction, and recall that we also have the outer inductive hypothesis
P(\rho,\tau)
. Then by the Product Constructor case, we have
P(\rho^{n+1},\tau)
.
Therefore,
C_{n}
holds for all
n
.

Now, for measurable
h\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\int h\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{List}\;\rho\times\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int h\,\mathrm{d}\!\left((\iota_{n}\times\mathsf{id}_{\tau})_{*}\,\nu_{U}^{\rho^{n}\times\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int h\,\mathrm{d}\!\left((\iota_{n}\times\mathsf{id}_{\tau})_{*}\,\mathsf{swap}_{*}\,\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\rho^{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\text{(by $C_{n}$)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int h\,\mathrm{d}\!\left(\mathsf{swap}_{*}(\mathsf{id}_{\tau}\times\iota_{n})_{*}\,\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\rho^{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int h\,\mathrm{d}\!\left(\mathsf{swap}_{*}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\mathsf{id}_{\tau}\times\iota_{n})_{*}\,\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\rho^{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int h\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{swap}_{*}\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\mathsf{List}\;\rho}).

Therefore
P(\mathsf{List}\;\rho,\tau)
holds (and by symmetry, so does
P(\tau,\mathsf{List}\;\rho)
).

(4)

NameMap constructor.

Assume
\sigma=\mathsf{NameMap}\;\rho
and
\tau
is some ground type.
By definition,

\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{NameMap}\;\rho}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\kappa_{n})_{*}\nu_{U}^{(\mathsf{Name}\times\rho)^{n}}.

where
\kappa_{n}:\left\llbracket(\mathsf{Name}\times\rho)^{n}\right\rrbracket\!\to\left\llbracket\mathsf{NameMap}\;\rho\right\rrbracket\!
are the coproduct injections.
As in the List case, using
N(\kappa_{n}(x))=N(x)
,

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{NameMap}\;\rho\times\tau}

\displaystyle=\left(a\leftsquigarrow\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\kappa_{n})_{*}\nu_{U}^{(\mathsf{Name}\times\rho)^{n}};\;z\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U\cup N(a)}^{\tau};\;\mathsf{ret}~(a,z)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\kappa_{n}\times\mathsf{id}_{\tau})_{*}\,\nu_{U}^{((\mathsf{Name}\times\rho)^{n})\times\tau},

\displaystyle\nu_{U}^{\tau\times\mathsf{NameMap}\;\rho}

\displaystyle=\left(z\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\tau};\;a\leftsquigarrow\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\kappa_{n})_{*}\nu_{U\cup N(z)}^{(\mathsf{Name}\times\rho)^{n}};\;\mathsf{ret}~(z,a)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\mathsf{id}_{\tau}\times\kappa_{n})_{*}\,\nu_{U}^{\tau\times(\mathsf{Name}\times\rho)^{n}}.

We can now repeat exactly the same argument as in the List case, but with
\rho
replaced by
(\mathsf{Name}\times\rho)
.

∎

B.2.
Logical Relation

In
Fig.

32
, for each type
\tau
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
,
we define a binary relation
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}\subseteq\underline{\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket\!}\times\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\right\rrbracket\!
,
where
\_:\nu\mathsf{Qbs}\to\mathsf{Set}
is the faithful functor given in
Corollary

A.5
. At ground types, the relation is the identity. At function types, we use a variant of the usual lifting of logical relations to function types, adapted to account for the fact that
\left\llbracket\tau\to_{\sigma}\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!
is a space of explicit closures. The interesting case is the one for probabilistic programs of type
\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\tau
. It relates the meaning of the
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
program,
(\mu,f)
, to the translation
(\gamma,\langle g,w\rangle)
when
g(\gamma,-)
is related to
f
, and
w(\gamma,-,U)
is the density
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{U}^{\sigma}}
.

15

15

There is not a case for
\mathsf{Dist}
, because in this appendix, we have identified
\mathsf{Dist}_{\kappa}\;\sigma
with
\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\{\}
, by the bijection
\mu\mapsto(\mu,!)
, where
!:\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket\to 1
is the unique map to the terminal object of
\nu\mathsf{Qbs}
.

With these relations defined, we establish the fundamental lemma:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{B}}

\displaystyle=\{(b,b^{\prime})\in\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{B}\mid b=b^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle=\{(r,r^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\mid r=r^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle=\{(n,n^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\mid n=n^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{Name}}

\displaystyle=\{(r_{1},r_{2})\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\mid r_{1}=r_{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\times\tau}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&((s_{1},s_{2}),(t_{1},t_{2}))\in\underline{\left\llbracket\sigma\times\tau\right\rrbracket\!}\times\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\sigma\times\tau\right\}\right\rrbracket\!\mid\\
&(s_{1},t_{1})\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma},(s_{2},t_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{List}\;\sigma}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&((n_{1},t_{1}),(n_{2},t_{2}))\in\left\llbracket\mathsf{List}\;\sigma\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\mathsf{List}\;\sigma\right\}\right\rrbracket\!\mid\\
&n_{1}=n_{2},\forall 1\leq i\leq n_{1},(\pi_{i}(t_{1}),\pi_{i}(t_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{NameMap}\;\tau}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&((n_{1},t_{1}),m)\in\left\llbracket\mathsf{NameMap}\;\tau\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\mathsf{NameMap}\;\tau\right\}\right\rrbracket\!\mid\\
&n_{1}=|\mathsf{dom}(m)|,\forall 1\leq i\leq n_{1},\pi_{1}(\pi_{i}(t_{1}))\in\mathsf{dom}(m)\wedge(\pi_{2}(\pi_{i}(t_{1})),m(\pi_{1}(\pi_{i}(t_{1}))))\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\tau}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&((\mu,f),(\gamma,\langle g,w\rangle))\in\underline{\left\llbracket\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\tau\right\rrbracket\!}\times\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\tau\right\}\right\rrbracket\!\mid\\
&((\rho,U)\mapsto f(\rho),(\gamma,g))\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\times\mathsf{NameSet}\to\tau},\\
&\forall\rho\in\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!,\forall U\supseteq\mathsf{supp}(\mu),w(\gamma,\rho,U)=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{U}^{\sigma}}(\rho)\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\tau{\to_{\sigma}}\tau^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&(f_{1},(\gamma,f_{2}))\in\left\llbracket\tau{\to_{\sigma}}\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!\times\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\tau{\to_{\sigma}}\tau^{\prime}\right\}\right\rrbracket\!\mid\\
&\forall(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau},(f_{1}(x_{1}),f_{2}(\gamma,x_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau^{\prime}}\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&(t_{1},t_{2})\in\underline{\left\llbracket\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}\right\rrbracket\!}\times\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}\right\}\right\rrbracket\!\mid\\
&\forall 1\leq i\leq n.\bigl(\pi_{(\ell_{i}:\tau_{i})}(t_{1}),\pi_{(\ell_{i}:\tau_{i})}(t_{2})\bigr)\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau_{i}}\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

Figure 32
.
The logical relation
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
.

Lemma B.2 (Fundamental Lemma).

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
in
\lambda_{\mathsf{gen}}
.
Then,
for all
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
,
it follows that
(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1}),\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
.

Proof Sketch.

The proof is by induction on
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
.
The cases
for variables, pairs, projections,
and function application
are standard. We give several illustrative cases
below.

Case: Lambda Terms

Suppose
\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp t:\tau\to_{\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma|_{\mathsf{FV}(\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp t)})}\tau^{\prime}

and let
f=\left\llbracket\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp{t}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1})
and
(\gamma,g)=\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp{t}\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2})
.
Consider any
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
.
Let
F:=\mathsf{FV}(\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}x\ldotp t)
.
By the
\lambda_{\mathsf{inc}}
closure construction semantics,
\gamma=\pi_{F}(\gamma_{2})
and

g(\gamma,s_{2})=\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\Gamma|_{F}\right\},x:\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\vdash\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}:\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\tau^{\prime}\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\pi_{F}(\gamma_{2}),s_{2}).

Since
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
, we have

(\pi_{F}(\gamma_{1}),\pi_{F}(\gamma_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma|_{F}}
.
Hence by the inductive hypothesis for
t
in context
\Gamma|_{F},x:\tau
, we obtain

\left(\left\llbracket\Gamma|_{F},x:\tau\vdash t:\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!(\pi_{F}(\gamma_{1}),s_{1}),\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\Gamma|_{F}\right\},x:\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\vdash\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}:\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\tau^{\prime}\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\pi_{F}(\gamma_{2}),s_{2})\right)\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau^{\prime}}.

Because
t
depends only on
F\cup\{x\}
, we have

\left\llbracket\Gamma,x:\tau\vdash t:\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1},s_{1})=\left\llbracket\Gamma|_{F},x:\tau\vdash t:\tau^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\!(\pi_{F}(\gamma_{1}),s_{1}).

Therefore, with the equality above for
g
,

(f(s_{1}),g(\gamma,s_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau^{\prime}}
.

Case: Return

Suppose
\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;t:\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma|_{\mathsf{FV}(t)})}\,\{\}\,\tau

and
let
(\mu,f)=\left\llbracket\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1})

and
(\gamma,\langle g,w\rangle)=\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} ret}}}\;t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2})
.
By the inductive hypothesis,

(t_{1},t_{2}):=(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1}),\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
.

•

For any
((s_{1},U_{1}),(s_{2},U_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\{\}\times\mathsf{NameSet}}
,
we have

(f(s_{1}),g(\gamma,s_{2},U_{2}))=(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
.

•

Recall
\mu=\delta_{\left\llbracket\{\}\right\rrbracket\!}
. For
\rho=\left\llbracket\{\}\right\rrbracket\!
and any
U
,
it follows that

\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\delta_{\left\llbracket\{\}\right\rrbracket\!}}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{U}^{\{\}}}\right)(\rho)=1=w(\gamma,\rho,U)
.

Case: Sample

Suppose
\Gamma\vdash\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t:\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma|_{\mathsf{FV}(t)})}\,\sigma\,\sigma

and let
(\mu,f)=\left\llbracket\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1})

and
(\gamma,\langle g,w\rangle)=\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2})
.
Let
((\mu^{\prime},f^{\prime}),(\gamma^{\prime},\langle g^{\prime},w^{\prime}\rangle)):=(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1}),\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\{\}}
(by the inductive hypothesis).
By the definition of
\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;t\right\}
, the translated sampler reuses the same density component as
\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}
, so

\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad w=w^{\prime}

(as functions of
(\rho,U)
).

•

Consider
((s_{1},U_{1}),(s_{2},U_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\times\mathsf{NameSet}}
.
By semantics of
\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} sample}}}\;\cdot
and its translation, for all traces
\rho
and name-sets
U
we have

f(\rho)=\rho
and
g(\gamma,\rho,U)=\rho
, hence

(f(s_{1}),g(\gamma,s_{2},U_{2}))=(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}.

•

For any
\rho\in\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\!
and
U\supseteq\mathsf{supp}(\mu)=\mathsf{supp}(\mu^{\prime})
,

\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{U}^{\sigma}}\right)(\rho)=\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{U}^{\sigma}}\right)(\rho)=w^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime},\rho,U)=w(\gamma,\rho,U).

Case: Let

Suppose
\Gamma\vdash x\leftarrow s;t:\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma|_{\mathsf{FV}(x\leftarrow s;t)})}\,\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}:\sigma_{m},\ell_{1}^{\prime}:\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{n}^{\prime}:\sigma_{n}^{\prime}\}\,\tau^{\prime}

and let
(\mu,f)=\left\llbracket x\leftarrow s;t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1})

and
(\gamma,\langle g,w\rangle)=\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{x\leftarrow s;t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2})
.
By the inductive hypothesis, let

((\mu_{1},f_{1}),(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\langle g_{1},w_{1}\rangle))=(\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1}),\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{s\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{P}_{\kappa^{\prime}}\,\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}:\sigma_{m}\}\,\tau}.

For each related pair
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
, define

((\mu_{2}^{v_{1}},f_{2}^{v_{1}}),(\gamma_{2}^{\prime v_{2}},\langle g_{2}^{v_{2}},w_{2}^{v_{2}}\rangle)):=(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1},v_{1}),\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2},v_{2})).

By the inductive hypothesis on
t
, each such pair lies in

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{P}_{\kappa^{\prime}}\,\{\ell_{1}^{\prime}:\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{n}^{\prime}:\sigma_{n}^{\prime}\}\,\tau^{\prime}}
.

•

For any
((s_{1},n_{1}),(s_{2},n_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}:\sigma_{m},\ell_{1}^{\prime}:\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{n}^{\prime}:\sigma_{n}^{\prime}\}\times\mathsf{NameSet}}
, let

v_{1}:=f_{1}(s_{1}|_{L_{1}}),\qquad v_{2}:=g_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}|_{L_{1}},n_{2}).

From the first conjunct of

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{P}_{\kappa^{\prime}}\,\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}:\sigma_{m}\}\,\tau}
, we get
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
.
Therefore, by the inductive hypothesis instance for
t
at
((\gamma_{1},v_{1}),(\gamma_{2},v_{2}))
,

\bigl(f_{2}^{v_{1}}(s_{1}|_{L_{2}}),\;g_{2}^{v_{2}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime v_{2}},s_{2}|_{L_{2}},n_{2}\cup\mathsf{names}(s_{2}|_{L_{1}}))\bigr)\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau^{\prime}}.

This is exactly the required functional part for
(f,g)
.

•

Consider any
U\supseteq\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
. We will proceed by reasoning in the monadic metalanguage
(
36
;
35
)
of the
\mathsf{Qbs}
measure monad
(
41
;
47
;
43
)
:

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho_{1}\leftsquigarrow\mu_{1};\\
&v=f_{1}(\rho_{1});\\
&\rho_{2}\leftsquigarrow\mu_{2}^{v};\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\rho_{1}\mathbin{+\!\!+}\rho_{2}\end{aligned}=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho_{1}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}:\sigma_{m}\}};\\
&\mathsf{score}(w_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\rho_{1},U));\\
&v=f_{1}(\rho_{1});\\
&v_{2}=g_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\rho_{1},U);\\
&\rho_{2}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U\cup\mathsf{names}(\rho_{1})}^{\{\ell_{1}^{\prime}:\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{n}^{\prime}:\sigma_{n}^{\prime}\}};\\
&\mathsf{score}(w_{2}^{v_{2}}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime v_{2}},\rho_{2},U\cup\mathsf{names}(\rho_{1})));\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\rho_{1}\mathbin{+\!\!+}\rho_{2}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho_{1}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}:\sigma_{m}\}};\\
&v=f_{1}(\rho_{1});\\
&v_{2}=g_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\rho_{1},U);\\
&\rho_{2}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U\cup\mathsf{names}(\rho_{1})}^{\{\ell_{1}^{\prime}:\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{n}^{\prime}:\sigma_{n}^{\prime}\}};\\
&\rho\leftsquigarrow\mathsf{ret}~\rho_{1}\mathbin{+\!\!+}\rho_{2};\\
&\mathsf{score}(w_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\rho|_{L_{1}},U)\cdot w_{2}^{g_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\rho|_{L_{1}},U)}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime g_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\rho|_{L_{1}},U)},\rho|_{L_{2}},U\cup\mathsf{names}(\rho|_{L_{1}})));\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\rho\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}:\sigma_{m},\ell_{1}^{\prime}:\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{n}^{\prime}:\sigma_{n}^{\prime}\}};\\
&\mathsf{score}(w_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\rho|_{L_{1}},U)\cdot w_{2}^{g_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\rho|_{L_{1}},U)}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime g_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\rho|_{L_{1}},U)},\rho|_{L_{2}},U\cup\mathsf{names}(\rho|_{L_{1}})));\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\rho\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}:\sigma_{m},\ell_{1}^{\prime}:\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{n}^{\prime}:\sigma_{n}^{\prime}\}};\\
&\mathsf{score}(w(\gamma,\rho,U));\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\rho\end{aligned}

The first equality unrolls the semantics of
x\leftarrow s;t
. The second uses the inductive hypothesis to rewrite draws from
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}^{v}
as compositions of the appropriate base measure and a score command, exploiting the fact that
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are correct densities. The third equality uses the commutativity of the Qbs measure monad to move all score commands to the end, and then exploits the identity that
\mathsf{score}(r_{1});\mathsf{score}(r_{2})=\mathsf{score}(r_{1}\cdot r_{2})
to combine them into one score command. Next, we use the definition of the reference measure for products (and thus records) to rewrite the three lines assigning
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
, and
\rho
into a single assignment of
\rho
from the joint reference measure (also relying on commutativity to bring the three lines together). Finally, we recognize the expression inside the
\mathsf{score}
command as being precisely
w(\gamma,\rho,U)
. We conclude that
w(\gamma,-,U)
is a density of
\mu
w.r.t.
\nu_{U}^{\{\ell_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}:\sigma_{m},\ell_{1}^{\prime}:\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{n}^{\prime}:\sigma_{n}^{\prime}\}}
, as desired.

Suppose
\Gamma\vdash t\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} @}}}\;\ell:\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma|_{\mathsf{FV}(t)})}\,\{\ell:\sigma\}\,\tau

and let

By the inductive hypothesis,
take

((\mu^{\prime},f^{\prime}),(\gamma^{\prime},\langle g^{\prime},w^{\prime}\rangle))=(\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1}),\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{t\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{P}_{\kappa}\,\sigma\,\tau}.

((s_{1},n_{1}),(s_{2},n_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\{\ell:\sigma\}\times\mathsf{NameSet}}

Consider any
U\supseteq\mathsf{supp}(\mu)

As with the previous case,

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho^{\prime}\leftsquigarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\{\ell:\rho^{\prime}\}\end{aligned}=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho^{\prime}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\sigma};\\
&\mathsf{score}(w^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},U));\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\{\ell:\rho^{\prime}\}\end{aligned}=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho^{\prime}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\sigma};\\

&\mathsf{ret}~\rho\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho\leftsquigarrow(\rho^{\prime}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\sigma};\mathsf{ret}~\{\ell:\rho^{\prime}\});\\
&\mathsf{score}(w^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime},\pi_{(\ell:\sigma)}(\rho),U));\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\rho\end{aligned}=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho\leftarrow\nu_{U}^{\{\ell:\sigma\}};\\
&\mathsf{score}(w(\gamma,\rho,U));\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\rho\end{aligned}

meaning
w
is a density of
\mu

w.r.t.
\nu_{U}^{\{\ell:\sigma\}}
.

Case: Loop (Simple)

We consider the special case

T=\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Range}}}\;d\;\{\;b\;\}\qquad\Gamma\vdash d:\mathsf{Dist}_{\kappa_{d}}\;\mathbb{N},\qquad\Gamma,x:\mathbb{N}\vdash b:\mathsf{P}_{\kappa_{b}}\,\sigma\,\tau,

with

\Gamma\vdash T:\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{CtxType}(\Gamma|_{\mathsf{FV}(T)})}\,(\mathsf{List}\;\sigma)\,(\mathsf{List}\;\tau).

This is the specialization of the loop rule in
Fig.

30
with no
using
clauses and a trivial accumulator.
In this case, the translation we are proving correct is:

\mathcal{W}\!\left\{\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} Range}}}\;d\;\{\;b\;\}\right\}=\left(\begin{aligned} \textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} $\bm{\lambda}$}}}(\mathsf{tr},\mathsf{U})\ldotp{}&\mathsf{N}:=\mathsf{length}\;\mathsf{tr};\;(\_,\mathsf{w}_{\mathsf{N}}):=\mathcal{W}\!\left\{d\right\}(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{U});\\
&\mathsf{ys}:=\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} for}}}\;x\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} in}}}\;\mathsf{range}\;\mathsf{N}\;\textnormal{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5} with}}}\;\mathsf{V}:=\mathsf{U}\;\{\;(r,w):=\mathcal{W}\!\left\{b\right\}(\mathsf{tr}[x],\mathsf{V});\\
&\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 8.50012pt((r,w),\mathsf{V}\cup\mathsf{names}(\mathsf{tr}[x]))\;\};\\
&(\mathsf{rs},\mathsf{ws}):=\mathsf{unzip}\;\mathsf{ys};\\
&(\mathsf{rs},\,\mathsf{w}_{\mathsf{N}}\cdot\mathsf{product}\;\mathsf{ws})\end{aligned}\right).

That is, the translated program computes the density of
d
at the length of the trace, and then multiplies that by the densities of the body subtraces.
Let

((\mu_{d},!),(\gamma_{d},\langle g_{d},w_{d}\rangle))=(\left\llbracket d\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1}),\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{d\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{P}_{\kappa_{d}}\,\mathbb{N}\,\{\}}.

Instantiating the inductive hypothesis for the body at any loop index
i\in\mathbb{N}
, in the extended related environments
(\gamma_{1},i)
and
(\gamma_{2},i)
, gives

((\mu_{i},f_{i}),(\gamma_{i},\langle g_{i},w_{i}\rangle))=(\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{1},i),\left\llbracket\mathcal{W}\!\left\{b\right\}\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma_{2},i))\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{P}_{\kappa_{b}}\,\sigma\,\tau}.

•

To show the return value function is correct, consider any

((\rho,U_{1}),(\rho^{\prime},U_{2}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{List}\;\sigma\times\mathsf{NameSet}}
.
Write
N=|\rho|=|\rho^{\prime}|
,
\rho_{i}=\pi_{i}(\rho)
, and
\rho_{i}^{\prime}=\pi_{i}(\rho^{\prime})
.
Define
V_{0}=U_{1}
,
V_{0}^{\prime}=U_{2}
and

V_{i}=V_{i-1}\cup\mathsf{names}(\rho_{i})
,
V_{i}^{\prime}=V_{i-1}^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{names}(\rho_{i}^{\prime})
.
By the list clause of the logical relation,
(\rho_{i},\rho_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}
for each
i
.
Since
(V_{i-1},V_{i-1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{NameSet}}
, applying the functional part of the body induction hypothesis at

((\rho_{i},V_{i-1}),(\rho_{i}^{\prime},V_{i-1}^{\prime}))
yields

(f_{i}(\rho_{i}),g_{i}(\gamma_{i},\rho_{i}^{\prime},V_{i-1}^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau}.

The source loop returns
(f_{i}(\rho_{i}))_{i=1}^{N}
, and the translated loop returns

(g_{i}(\gamma_{i},\rho_{i}^{\prime},V_{i-1}^{\prime}))_{i=1}^{N}
.
Hence the two results are related by
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{List}\;\tau}
.

•

To show the density correct, let
U\supseteq\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
, where
\mu
is the trace distribution of
T
.
We write
V_{0}=U
and, for a length-
N
trace
\rho=(\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{N})
,

V_{i}=V_{i-1}\cup\mathsf{names}(\rho_{i}).

By the inductive hypotheses,

w_{d}(\gamma_{d},N,U)
is the density of
\mu_{d}
w.r.t.
\nu_{U}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and

w_{i}(\gamma_{i},\rho_{i},V_{i-1})
is the density of
\mu_{i}
with respect to
\nu_{V_{i-1}}^{\sigma}
.
Therefore, in the monadic metalanguage,

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &N\leftsquigarrow\mu_{d};\\
&\rho_{1}\leftsquigarrow\mu_{1};\ \dots\ ;\rho_{N}\leftsquigarrow\mu_{N};\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\iota_{N}(\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{N})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &N\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\mathbb{N}};\\
&\rho_{1}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\sigma};\\
&\rho_{2}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{V_{1}}^{\sigma};\ \dots\ ;\rho_{N}\leftsquigarrow\nu_{V_{N-1}}^{\sigma};\\
&\mathsf{score}\!\left(w_{d}(\gamma_{d},N,U)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}(\gamma_{i},\rho_{i},V_{i-1})\right);\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\iota_{N}(\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{N})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\cdot\vdash\begin{aligned} &\rho\leftsquigarrow\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{List}\;\sigma};\\
&\mathsf{score}(w(\gamma,\rho,U));\\
&\mathsf{ret}~\rho.\end{aligned}

The second equality uses the body and bound induction hypotheses and combines the resulting score commands.
The last equality uses the definition

\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{List}\;\sigma}=\sum_{N\in\mathbb{N}}(\iota_{N})_{*}\nu_{U}^{\sigma^{N}}
, where

\nu_{U}^{\sigma^{N}}
is the iterated product measure that samples the
i
th component from
\nu_{V_{i-1}}^{\sigma}
.
The score expression is exactly the weight computed by the specialized translation of
T
.
Thus
w(\gamma,-,U)
is a density of
\mu
with respect to
\nu_{U}^{\mathsf{List}\;\sigma}
.

∎

Appendix C
Incrementalizing Transformation: Full Definition and Correctness

The full type and term translations are given in
Figs.

33
and
34
, and the full version of the for-expression translation is given in
Fig.

35
. The full definitions of the change-validity relation and logical relation are given in
Figs.

36
and
37
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}

\displaystyle=\sigma\quad\text{ for ground types }\sigma

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\times\tau\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}\times\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\mathsf{List}\;\sigma\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{List}\;\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\mathsf{NameMap}\;\sigma\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{NameMap}\;\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\{\ell_{1}:\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau_{1}\right\},\dots,\ell_{n}:\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau_{n}\right\}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}\to\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\tau\right\}\times\mathsf{Upd}(\Delta(\kappa\times\sigma);\Delta(\tau))

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\Gamma\right\}

\displaystyle=(x_{1}:\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma_{1}\right\},\dots,x_{n}:\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma_{n}\right\})\text{ for contexts }\Gamma=(x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\sigma_{n})

\displaystyle\Delta(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\textnormal{new}:\sigma,\textnormal{same}:\mathbb{B}\}\quad\text{ for }\sigma=\mathbb{B},\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R},\mathsf{Name}

\displaystyle\Delta(\sigma\times\tau)

\displaystyle=\Delta(\sigma)\times\Delta(\tau)

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathsf{List}\;\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\textnormal{insert}:\mathsf{List}\;(\mathbb{N}\times\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\}),\textnormal{remove}:\mathsf{List}\;\mathbb{N},\textnormal{change}:\mathsf{List}\;(\mathbb{N}\times\Delta(\sigma))\}

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathsf{NameMap}\;\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\textnormal{add}:\mathsf{NameMap}\;\mathcal{I}\!\left\{\sigma\right\},\textnormal{remove}:\mathsf{NameSet},\textnormal{change}:\mathsf{NameMap}\;\Delta(\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\Delta(\{\ell_{1}:\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}:\tau_{n}\})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Sub}\{\ell_{1}:\Delta(\tau_{1}),\dots,\ell_{n}:\Delta(\tau_{n})\}

\displaystyle\Delta(\sigma\to_{\kappa}\tau)

\displaystyle=\Delta(\kappa)

\displaystyle\Delta(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=(x_{1}:\Delta(\sigma_{1}),\dots,x_{n}:\Delta(\sigma_{n}))\text{ for contexts }\Gamma=(x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\sigma_{n})
